The money, time or effort is spent whatever we do next. Rational choice looks only at future costs and benefits. Recognising sunk costs can free us to leave bad investments, jobs or plans.
ideamind|The Availability Heuristic|We judge how likely something is by how easily examples come to mind, so vivid, recent or frightening events seem more common than they are.|Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman||e|1973|Tversky and Kahneman found that people judge frequency by ease of recall. After seeing news of a plane crash, flying feels more dangerous than driving, though cars are far deadlier. Dramatic, emotional or recent events stick in memory. Checking actual statistics, rather than relying on what comes to mind, gives a more accurate picture.
ideamind|The Bystander Effect|The more people who witness an emergency, the less likely any one of them is to help: each assumes someone else will. Knowing this makes it easier to step forward.|John Darley and Bibb Latané||e|1968|The research followed the 1964 murder of Kitty Genovese in New York, which newspapers reported, with exaggeration, as witnessed by 38 passive bystanders. Darley and Latané's experiments showed that people in groups help less, because responsibility is diffused. The remedy is to act as though you are the only one, or to point to a specific person and ask for help.
ideamind|The Peak–End Rule|We remember an experience mostly by its most intense moment and how it ended, not by its length. A good ending can redeem a hard day.|Daniel Kahneman and others||e|1993|In one study led by Daniel Kahneman, people preferred a longer painful experience if its ending was less painful. We remember experiences by their peak and their end rather than total duration. The rule applies to holidays, medical procedures and relationships. Ending a hard day, a meeting or a visit on a kind note shapes how it is remembered.
ideamind|The Zeigarnik Effect|Unfinished tasks stay on our minds more than finished ones. Starting something, even a little, keeps it alive in the mind.|Bluma Zeigarnik||e|1927|Bluma Zeigarnik, a Soviet psychologist, studied after her teacher Kurt Lewin noticed that waiters remembered unpaid orders better than paid ones. She found people recalled interrupted tasks better than completed ones. Later studies found the effect is not always reliable, but it has inspired techniques for beating procrastination and writing cliffhangers.
ideamind|Fundamental Attribution Error|We explain others' failings by their character ("he's careless") but our own by circumstances ("I was rushed"). Charity toward others corrects the balance.|Lee Ross||e|1977|Lee Ross named this tendency to over-attribute others' behaviour to their personality and underweight the situation. Someone cuts in front of us in traffic and we think they're rude, not that they might be rushing to hospital. When we err, we see our circumstances clearly. Remembering others face hidden pressures encourages fairness and empathy.
ideamind|Attachment|The bond a child forms with its caregivers becomes a template for later love: a secure base from which to explore, or an anxious one.|John Bowlby and Mary Ainsworth||e|1950s–1970s|John Bowlby, a British psychiatrist, argued that infants have an innate need to bond with caregivers for safety. Mary Ainsworth's Strange Situation experiments identified secure and insecure attachment styles. Securely attached children explore confidently, knowing comfort is available. Adults show similar patterns in relationships, though attachment styles can change through new experiences.
ideamind|Self-Determination|People thrive when three needs are met: autonomy (choosing for ourselves), competence (getting good at things) and relatedness (belonging).|Edward Deci and Richard Ryan||e|1985|Edward Deci and Richard Ryan found that rewards can actually reduce motivation for activities people already enjoy. Their self-determination theory holds that lasting motivation comes from within when three basic needs are satisfied. Schools, workplaces and families that support choice, growth and connection see people flourish; controlling environments tend to drain them.
ideamind|The Impostor Phenomenon|Many capable people feel they are frauds about to be found out, crediting success to luck. The feeling is common, and especially common among the competent.|Pauline Clance and Suzanne Imes||e|1978|Psychologists Pauline Clance and Suzanne Imes first described it among high-achieving women, though it affects people of all backgrounds. Sufferers dismiss evidence of ability and fear exposure. It often comes with perfectionism. Talking about it helps, since many discover respected colleagues feel the same, and noticing the gap between feelings and evidence can ease its grip.
ideasoc|Feared or Loved|Upon this a question arises: whether it be better to be loved than feared or feared than loved? It may be answered that one should wish to be both, but, because it is difficult to unite them in one person, it is much safer to be feared than loved, when, of the two, either must be dispensed with.|Niccolò Machiavelli, The Prince (tr. W. K. Marriott)|Machiavelli looked at power as it is, not as it should be. His cold realism founded modern political science and still disturbs readers.|q||Niccolò Machiavelli wrote The Prince in 1513 after being ousted from office in Florence. He advised rulers to be ready to act ruthlessly when needed, since being feared is safer than being loved, though they must avoid being hated. Machiavellian now means manipulative, but scholars debate whether he was cynical, satirical or simply realistic about politics.
ideasoc|The Strong and the Weak|Right, as the world goes, is only in question between equals in power, while the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must.|Thucydides, The History of the Peloponnesian War (tr. Richard Crawley)|The Athenians' words to the Melians before destroying them: justice only matters between equals. Thucydides records it as a warning about power without restraint.|q||In 416 BC Athens demanded that the small island of Melos submit. Thucydides, an Athenian general and historian, reconstructed their debate. The Melians appealed to justice and the gods; the Athenians answered with power alone. Melos refused and was destroyed. The Melian Dialogue is still read as a lesson in power politics and its moral costs.
ideasoc|The Protestant Ethic|Weber argued that a religious drive to prove oneself through disciplined work helped create modern capitalism, which later became an "iron cage" of efficiency without meaning.|Max Weber||e|1905|Max Weber, a German sociologist, argued in 1905 that Calvinist Protestants sought signs of God's favour through hard work, thrift and success, creating the disciplined mindset capitalism needed. Over time the religious meaning faded, leaving the drive to work and accumulate. His iron cage image describes modern life ruled by rational efficiency without deeper purpose.
ideasoc|Anomie|When a society's norms break down or no longer fit people's lives, individuals lose their bearings. Durkheim linked this rootlessness to despair.|Émile Durkheim||e|1893|Émile Durkheim, a founder of sociology, used anomie, meaning normlessness, to describe societies in rapid change where shared rules weaken. In his 1897 study Suicide, he found higher rates in times of economic upheaval and in groups with weak social bonds. His work showed that seemingly personal crises have social causes too.
ideasoc|The Matthew Effect|Advantage accumulates: the already recognised get more credit, the rich more opportunity. "To him that hath shall be given."|Robert K. Merton||e|1968|The sociologist Robert K. Merton named it after the Gospel of Matthew: unto every one that hath shall be given. He noticed famous scientists get more credit than unknown collaborators for similar work. The effect appears in wealth, education and online popularity, where early advantages snowball. Recognising it helps design fairer systems.
ideasoc|Social Capital|Networks of trust and cooperation between people are a kind of wealth. Communities rich in them are healthier, safer and more prosperous.|Robert Putnam and others||e|1990s|Robert Putnam's book Bowling Alone, from 2000, documented the decline of clubs, churches and neighbourly ties in America. He argued social capital, the trust and reciprocity created by such networks, makes democracy and communities work. Places with more of it have lower crime, better health and stronger economies. Rebuilding it is a challenge for modern societies.
ideasoc|Externalities|Some costs and benefits fall on people outside a deal, like pollution or a neighbour's beautiful garden. Markets ignore them unless rules bring them in.|Arthur Pigou||e|1920|Arthur Pigou, a British economist, described costs or benefits that fall on third parties. A factory's pollution harms neighbours who never agreed to it; a beekeeper's bees pollinate nearby orchards for free. Since markets don't price these effects, Pigou proposed taxes or subsidies to correct them. Carbon taxes are a modern example.
ideasoc|Moral Hazard|When we are protected from the consequences of a risk, we tend to take more of it.|insurance theory||e|19th century|The term arose in the insurance industry, which noticed that insured people sometimes take less care, since losses will be covered. The idea became central to economics after the 2008 financial crisis, when banks expecting government bailouts were accused of reckless risk-taking. Designing systems where people bear some consequences helps limit it.
ideasoc|Gresham's Law|When debased and good money circulate together, people hoard the good and spend the bad, until only the bad remains. Bad drives out good.|Thomas Gresham||e|16th century|Named after Sir Thomas Gresham, a financier to Queen Elizabeth I, though the observation is older. When coins with less precious metal have the same official value as pure ones, people keep the good coins and spend the bad ones, so bad money circulates. The phrase is also used loosely, for example about bad information crowding out good.
ideasoc|The Overton Window|At any time only a range of ideas seems politically acceptable. That window can shift, making the once unthinkable ordinary, or the reverse.|Joseph Overton||e|1990s|Joseph Overton, a policy analyst, described how politicians can only support policies within the range the public currently accepts. Activists, writers and events can move the window by making ideas more familiar, turning the radical into the reasonable. The concept explains how social attitudes on many issues have changed rapidly.
ideamath|Zeno's Paradoxes|To cross a room you must first cross half, then half the rest, and so on forever. How does motion ever finish? The answer had to wait for the mathematics of infinite sums.|Zeno of Elea||e|5th century BC|Zeno of Elea, a Greek philosopher, devised paradoxes to support his teacher Parmenides' claim that change is an illusion. In Achilles and the Tortoise, the fast runner can never overtake a tortoise with a head start, since he must first reach where it was. Calculus and the idea of convergent infinite series later showed how infinitely many steps can add up to a finite total.
ideamath|The Monty Hall Problem|Three doors, one prize. You pick one; the host, who knows, opens another to show nothing. Should you switch? Yes: switching wins two times in three, though intuition insists it's even.|Steve Selvin||e|1975|Named after the host of the TV game show Let's Make a Deal, the puzzle became famous in 1990 when columnist Marilyn vos Savant gave the correct answer and thousands of readers, including mathematicians, wrote to say she was wrong. The host's choice gives information: your first pick has a one in three chance, so switching wins two in three.
ideamath|The Birthday Paradox|In a room of just 23 people, the chance that two share a birthday is better than even. Coincidences are far more common than we expect.|Richard von Mises||e|1939|It feels surprising because we think about our own birthday matching someone else's, which is unlikely. But with 23 people there are 253 possible pairs, and each could match. With 70 people, the probability rises above 99.9 per cent. It reminds us that surprising coincidences are statistically ordinary.
ideamath|The Fibonacci Sequence|1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13: each number the sum of the two before. The pattern turns up in sunflower seeds, pine cones and shells.|Leonardo of Pisa (Fibonacci)||e|1202|Leonardo of Pisa, known as Fibonacci, introduced the sequence to Europe in his book Liber Abaci in 1202, through a puzzle about breeding rabbits; Indian mathematicians had known it earlier. The ratio between consecutive numbers approaches the golden ratio. Its appearance in plant growth reflects efficient packing of seeds and leaves.
ideamath|The Pigeonhole Principle|Put more pigeons than holes and some hole must hold two. This obvious fact proves surprising things, such as that two people in London have exactly the same number of hairs.|Peter Dirichlet||e|1834|The German mathematician Peter Gustav Lejeune Dirichlet used this simple principle in number theory. Since there are more people in London than possible numbers of hairs on a head, at least two must have exactly the same count. It is used in computer science to prove that no compression method can shrink every file.
ideamath|Calculus|By looking at change in infinitely small steps, calculus describes motion, growth and curves exactly. It is the language of physics and engineering.|Isaac Newton and Gottfried Leibniz||e|1660s–1680s|Isaac Newton and Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz independently developed calculus in the late 17th century, sparking a bitter priority dispute. Its two halves, differentiation and integration, measure rates of change and accumulate small parts into wholes. It let Newton describe planetary motion and is now essential in physics, engineering, economics and medicine.
ideamath|Fractals|Shapes that repeat their pattern at every scale, like coastlines, ferns and clouds. Nature's roughness has its own precise geometry.|Benoît Mandelbrot||e|1975|Benoit Mandelbrot, a Polish-born French-American mathematician, coined the term fractal in 1975, from the Latin for broken. He showed that coastlines, mountains and clouds have self-similar patterns: zoom in and you see similar shapes. The Mandelbrot set, generated by a simple formula, has infinitely intricate boundaries. Fractals are used in graphics, antennas and medicine.
ideamath|Equilibrium|In a game where everyone has chosen their best response to the others, no one gains by changing alone. Such a balance can still leave everyone worse off.|John Nash||e|1950|John Nash, an American mathematician, proved in 1950 that every finite game has at least one such equilibrium. It became a cornerstone of game theory, used in economics, biology and politics, and earned him a Nobel Prize in 1994. His life, including his struggle with schizophrenia, was portrayed in the film A Beautiful Mind.
ideaart|Life Imitates Art|Paradox though it may seem—and paradoxes are always dangerous things—it is none the less true that Life imitates art far more than Art imitates life.|Oscar Wilde, The Decay of Lying|Wilde's paradox: we see the world through the art we know. There were fogs in London before the painters, he says, but no one noticed them.|q||In his 1889 dialogue The Decay of Lying, Oscar Wilde playfully argued that nature and life copy art, not the reverse. People see sunsets differently after painters have shown them, and young people imitate characters from novels. The paradox makes a serious point: art shapes the categories through which we perceive and live.
ideaart|The Hard, Gemlike Flame|To burn always with this hard, gemlike flame, to maintain this ecstasy, is success in life.|Walter Pater, The Renaissance|Life is brief and every moment unique, so the art of living is intensity of attention, to beauty, thought and feeling, before it all melts away.|q||Walter Pater, an Oxford critic, wrote this in the conclusion to his 1873 book on the Renaissance. Since life is a series of fleeting impressions, he urged readers to seek the most intense experiences of beauty and thought. The passage inspired the aesthetic movement and Oscar Wilde, though Pater worried it could be misread as mere pleasure-seeking.
ideaart|The Willing Suspension of Disbelief|That willing suspension of disbelief for the moment, which constitutes poetic faith.|Samuel Taylor Coleridge, Biographia Literaria|To enjoy a story we set aside our knowledge that it isn't real. Coleridge called that generous choice "poetic faith".|q||Samuel Taylor Coleridge coined the phrase in his Biographia Literaria in 1817, explaining how readers could accept supernatural elements in poems like his Rime of the Ancient Mariner. As long as a story has human truth, we agree to set aside our scepticism. This voluntary belief is what allows fiction, theatre and film to move us.
ideaart|Emotion Recollected in Tranquillity|Poetry is the spontaneous overflow of powerful feelings: it takes its origin from emotion recollected in tranquillity.|William Wordsworth, Preface to Lyrical Ballads|Poems begin in strong feeling, but are written when we can look back on it calmly and let it come alive again.|q||Wordsworth's preface to the second edition of Lyrical Ballads, in 1800, was a manifesto of English Romanticism. Poetry, he argued, should use the real language of ordinary people and come from genuine feeling. But writing requires distance: the poet recalls emotion in calm reflection until it stirs again and shapes itself into verse.
ideaart|Art as Infection|Tolstoy defined art as passing on a feeling you have lived through so that others feel it too. Good art unites people in shared feeling.|Leo Tolstoy||e|1897|In his 1897 essay What Is Art?, Leo Tolstoy rejected definitions based on beauty. Art, he said, is one person consciously conveying feelings to others through external signs, so that they are infected by them. Sincere, clear art that unites people is good; elite, confusing art is bad. He even condemned some of his own earlier works by this standard.
ideaart|Inner Necessity|Kandinsky held that colour and form act directly on the soul, like music, and that true art comes from inner necessity, not from copying the outer world.|Wassily Kandinsky||e|1911|Wassily Kandinsky, a Russian painter, wrote Concerning the Spiritual in Art in 1911, around when he painted some of the first abstract works. He believed colours have spiritual effects, yellow warm and aggressive, blue deep and calm, and that painting could move us directly like music. Art should express the artist's inner necessity rather than imitate nature.
ideaart|The Play Drive|Schiller argued we are fully human only when we play: in art, sense and reason meet freely, and beauty educates us toward freedom.|Friedrich Schiller||e|1795|Friedrich Schiller, a German poet and philosopher, wrote Letters on the Aesthetic Education of Man in 1795, after the violence of the French Revolution. He argued that humans are torn between sensuous and rational drives, which are reconciled in play and the experience of beauty. Aesthetic education, not force, would prepare people for political freedom.
ideaart|Show, Don't Tell|Let the reader see the glint of light on broken glass rather than being told the moon is shining. Detail persuades where statement doesn't.|after Anton Chekhov||e|1886|The advice is usually traced to a letter Chekhov wrote to his brother in 1886, though the famous phrasing is a later paraphrase. Instead of stating emotions or facts directly, writers can present concrete details and actions that let readers infer them. It engages the imagination and makes stories feel real, though sometimes telling is the right choice.
mythgreek|The Golden Age|First of all the deathless gods who dwell on Olympus made a golden race of mortal men who lived in the time of Cronos when he was reigning in heaven. And they lived like gods without sorrow of heart, remote and free from toil and grief: miserable age rested not on them; but with legs and arms never failing they made merry with feasting beyond the reach of all evils. When they died, it was as though they were overcome with sleep, and they had all good things; for the fruitful earth unforced bare them fruit abundantly and without stint.|Hesiod, Works and Days (tr. Hugh G. Evelyn-White)|Hesiod's first of five ages: people lived like gods, without toil or old age. Every culture seems to remember a lost golden time, and to measure the present against it.|q||Hesiod's Works and Days describes five ages of humanity: golden, silver, bronze, heroic and iron, our own troubled time. In the golden age people lived without toil, sorrow or old age, and died as if falling asleep. Similar myths appear in Hindu, Roman and other traditions. They express nostalgia, but also a vision of how life might be.
mythgreek|Apollo and Daphne|With the leaden shaft he struck the nymph Daphne, the daughter of the river god Peneus, and with the golden one Apollo, through the heart.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Cupid's two arrows: one makes Apollo love, the other makes Daphne flee. She becomes a laurel tree rather than be caught, and the laurel becomes Apollo's crown: desire turned into honour.|q||Ovid's Metamorphoses tells how Apollo mocked Cupid's archery, so Cupid shot him with a golden arrow of love and the nymph Daphne with a lead arrow of aversion. Apollo pursued her until she prayed for rescue and was transformed into a laurel tree. Apollo made the laurel his sacred tree, and laurel wreaths crowned poets and victors.
mythgreek|Pyramus and Thisbe|In the wall that parted the two houses there was a crack, caused by some fault in the structure. No one had remarked it before, but the lovers discovered it. What will not love discover! It afforded a passage to the voice; and tender messages used to pass backward and forward through the gap. As they stood, Pyramus on this side, Thisbe on that, their breaths would mingle.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Two young lovers kept apart by their parents whisper through a crack in a wall. Their tragic mistake inspired Romeo and Juliet.|q||In Ovid's tale, set in Babylon, the young lovers arranged to meet at a mulberry tree. Thisbe, frightened by a lioness, fled and dropped her veil, which the lion bloodied. Pyramus found it, believed her dead and killed himself; Thisbe returned and did the same. The mulberry's fruit turned dark red. Shakespeare used it in Romeo and Juliet and parodied it in A Midsummer Night's Dream.
mythgreek|Theseus and the Thread|She furnished him with a sword, with which to encounter the Minotaur, and with a clew of thread by which he might find his way out of the labyrinth. He was successful, slew the Minotaur, escaped from the labyrinth, and taking Ariadne as the companion of his way, with his rescued companions sailed for Athens.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Ariadne's ball of thread leads Theseus out of the Labyrinth. Courage is not enough; we also need a way back, and often someone who gives it to us.|q||Every few years Athens had to send young people to Crete to be devoured by the Minotaur, a monster in a maze built by Daedalus. Theseus volunteered. Princess Ariadne fell in love with him and gave him a ball of thread to unroll and follow back out. He killed the Minotaur and escaped, though he later abandoned Ariadne on an island.
mythgreek|Cassandra|Cassandra had been loved by Apollo, and he gave her the gift of prophecy; but afterwards offended with her, he rendered the gift unavailing by ordaining that her predictions should never be believed.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Cursed to see the truth and never be believed. Cassandra has become the name for every unheeded warning.|q||Cassandra was a princess of Troy. Apollo gave her prophecy to win her love, and when she refused him, he cursed her never to be believed. She warned the Trojans about the wooden horse, but they ignored her, and the city fell. Today a Cassandra is anyone whose accurate warnings are dismissed, from scientists to whistleblowers.
mythgreek|Isis Seeks Osiris|When Isis heard of the cruel murder she wept and mourned, and then with her hair shorn, clothed in black and beating her breast, she sought diligently for the body of her husband.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Isis searches the world for her murdered husband and brings him back. Grief that will not give up becomes a power over death.|q||Bulfinch retells the Egyptian myth: after Set trapped Osiris in a chest and threw it into the Nile, Isis searched until she found it at Byblos. Set then tore the body into pieces and scattered them; Isis gathered them again. Her devotion made her one of the most widely worshipped goddesses of the ancient world, honoured across the Roman Empire.
mythgreek|Penelope's Web|We could see her working on her great web all day long, but at night she would unpick the stitches again by torchlight. She fooled us in this way for three years and we never found her out.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book II (tr. Samuel Butler)|Penelope keeps the suitors waiting by weaving by day and unpicking by night. Faithfulness here is not passive waiting but cleverness and endurance.|q||In the Odyssey, while Odysseus spent twenty years at war and wandering, suitors besieged his wife Penelope, demanding she remarry. She promised to choose once she finished weaving a burial shroud for her father-in-law, then secretly undid each day's work. Her intelligence and constancy make her one of literature's great heroines, Odysseus's equal in cunning.
mythgreek|The Sirens|First you will come to the Sirens who enchant all who come near them. If any one unwarily draws in too close and hears the singing of the Sirens, his wife and children will never welcome him home again, for they sit in a green field and warble him to death with the sweetness of their song.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book XII (tr. Samuel Butler)|The Sirens' song promises everything and leads to death. Odysseus has himself tied to the mast so he can hear it without being lost: knowing our weaknesses, we can plan around them.|q||The witch Circe warned Odysseus about the Sirens, whose song lured sailors onto rocks. He plugged his crew's ears with wax and had himself bound to the mast, ordering them to ignore his pleas. Psychologists cite this as a classic example of precommitment: arranging our circumstances in advance to resist future temptations.
mythgreek|Argos the Faithful Dog|As soon as he saw Ulysses standing there, he dropped his ears and wagged his tail, but he could not get close up to his master. When Ulysses saw the dog on the other side of the yard, he dashed a tear from his eyes without Eumaeus seeing it.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book XVII (tr. Samuel Butler)|After twenty years, only the old dog recognises Odysseus in disguise, and dies having seen him. One of literature's most tender moments of loyalty.|q||When Odysseus finally returned to Ithaca disguised as a beggar, his old dog Argos, neglected and lying on a dung heap, recognised him immediately. Odysseus, unable to reveal himself, secretly wiped away a tear as he passed. Argos then died, having lived to see his master home. The scene is a moving portrait of loyalty and time.
mythgreek|Niobe|Alas! how different was this Niobe from her who had so lately driven away the people from the sacred rites, and held her stately course through the city, the envy of her friends, now the pity even of her foes!|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|A mother boasts that her fourteen children make her greater than a goddess and loses them all. She weeps until she turns to stone, and the stone still weeps.|q||Niobe, queen of Thebes, boasted that her seven sons and seven daughters made her superior to Leto, who had only two children, Apollo and Artemis. The divine twins killed all of Niobe's children with arrows. Overwhelmed by grief, she turned to stone on Mount Sipylus, where a rock formation said to resemble a weeping woman still seeps water.
mythnorse|The First Humans|Then from the throng did three come forth,\nFrom the home of the gods, the mighty and gracious;\nTwo without fate on the land they found,\nAsk and Embla, empty of might.\nSoul they had not, sense they had not,\nHeat nor motion, nor goodly hue;\nSoul gave Othin, sense gave Hönir,\nHeat gave Lothur and goodly hue.|The Poetic Edda, Voluspo (tr. Henry Adams Bellows)|The gods find two trees on the shore, Ask and Embla, and give them breath, mind and warmth: the first man and woman, made from wood.|q||In the Voluspo, the gods Odin, Hoenir and Lodur found two lifeless trees, an ash and an elm, on the shore. Odin gave them breath and soul, Hoenir sense, and Lodur warmth and colour. Ask and Embla became the ancestors of humanity in Midgard. The myth links people to trees, echoing the importance of Yggdrasil.
mythnorse|Odin's Eye|I know where Othin’s eye is hidden,\nDeep in the wide-famed well of Mimir;\nMead from the pledge of Othin each morn\nDoes Mimir drink: would you know yet more?|The Poetic Edda, Voluspo (tr. Henry Adams Bellows)|Odin gave one of his eyes to drink from Mimir's well of wisdom. Insight, the myth says, costs something of our ordinary sight.|q||Mimir's well, beneath one of Yggdrasil's roots, held wisdom and knowledge. Odin, always seeking understanding, sacrificed one eye to drink from it. That is why he is often depicted with one eye. The myth suggests deep wisdom requires sacrifice and perhaps a different way of seeing, inward rather than outward.
mythnorse|Thor Wrestles Old Age|It was also a most astonishing feat when you wrestled with Elle, for none has ever been, and none shall ever be, that Elle (eld, old age) will not get the better of him, though he gets to be old enough to abide her coming.|The Prose Edda (tr. Rasmus B. Anderson)|The mighty Thor is brought to one knee by a frail old woman, who turns out to be Old Age herself. No one, however strong, defeats her.|q||In the Prose Edda, Thor and his companions visit the giant Utgarda-Loki, who sets challenges. Thor fails to lift a cat, drain a drinking horn and wrestle down an old nurse named Elli. Only afterwards does he learn these were illusions: the cat was the world serpent, the horn connected to the sea, and Elli was old age, which no one can defeat.
mythancient|The Gift of the Nile|Isis showed them first the use of wheat and barley, and Osiris made the instruments of agriculture and taught men the use of them, as well as how to harness the ox to the plough.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Isis and Osiris were remembered as the gods who gave Egypt farming and civilisation, tied to the Nile's yearly flood that made the desert bloom.|q||For ancient Egyptians, the annual flooding of the Nile brought rich silt that made farming possible in the desert. The historian Herodotus called Egypt the gift of the Nile. Osiris was linked with the flood and the growth of grain, and Isis with fertility. Their story connected the agricultural cycle with death and rebirth.
mythworld|Nachiketas and Death|The good is one thing, the pleasant another; these two, having different objects, chain a man. It is well with him who clings to the good; he who chooses the pleasant, misses his end.|The Upanishads, Katha (tr. F. Max Müller)|A boy sent to the house of Death refuses all the riches Death offers and insists on learning what lies beyond. Death, impressed, teaches him the difference between the good and the merely pleasant.|q||In the Katha Upanishad, the boy Nachiketas is sent to Yama, god of death, by his angry father. Yama, absent for three days, offers him three wishes. For the third, Nachiketas asks what happens after death. Yama tries to tempt him with wealth and long life, but the boy refuses, and Yama teaches him about the eternal self, Atman.
mythworld|The One Transformed|As milk changes to curd, and water to ice, so is Brahma variously transformed and diversified, without aid of exterior means of any sort.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|In Hindu thought, the one reality, Brahman, takes all the forms of the world as milk becomes curd, without ceasing to be itself.|q||Bulfinch, writing in the 1850s for Western readers, summarised Hindu teachings that the universe is a manifestation of Brahma, the supreme reality. Just as milk naturally turns into curd and water into ice, the one becomes the many without any outside help. The image expresses the Vedantic vision that all diversity is unity in different forms.
mythworld|The Children of Lir|No friends and no power that you have will be able to bring you out of these shapes you are in through the length of your lives, until you have been three hundred years on Loch Dairbhreach, and three hundred years on Sruth na Maoile between Ireland and Alban, and three hundred years at Irrus Domnann and Inis Gluaire.|Lady Gregory, Gods and Fighting Men|A jealous stepmother turns Lir's four children into swans for nine hundred years. They keep their voices and sing so beautifully that all who hear them find comfort: beauty survives even a curse.|q||This Irish legend tells how Aoife, jealous of her stepchildren's love with their father Lir, turned them into swans, bound to spend three hundred years on each of three waters. They kept their human voices and sang haunting music. At the end, hearing a Christian bell, they returned to human form as very old people and died. It is one of Ireland's best-loved stories.
mythworld|Rhiannon|I am Rhiannon, the daughter of Heveydd Hên, and they sought to give me to a husband against my will. But no husband would I have, and that because of my love for thee, neither will I yet have one unless thou reject me. And hither have I come to hear thy answer.” “By Heaven,” said Pwyll, “behold this is my answer. If I might choose among all the ladies and damsels in the world, thee would I choose.|The Mabinogion, Pwyll Prince of Dyved (tr. Lady Charlotte Guest)|A woman on a white horse whom no rider can catch, until Pwyll simply asks her to stop. Rhiannon chooses her own husband, a rare thing in old tales.|q||In the Mabinogion, the Welsh prince Pwyll sees a lady on a white horse; though she seems to ride slowly, none of his horsemen can catch her. Finally he calls out, and she stops and explains she has come to marry him rather than an unwanted suitor. Rhiannon is later falsely accused of harming her child and bears her punishment with dignity.
mythworld|Oisín in the Land of Youth|Oisín rides with the fairy Niamh to Tír na nÓg, the land of eternal youth. After what seem three years he returns to Ireland, finds three hundred have passed, touches the ground and becomes an old man.|Irish myth||e|Irish legend|Oisín, a poet and warrior of the Fianna, was invited by Niamh of the Golden Hair to the magical land of Tír na nÓg. After three happy years, he longed to visit home. Niamh warned him not to touch the ground. In Ireland he found his friends long dead. Helping men lift a stone, he fell from his horse and aged three centuries in an instant.
mythworld|The Tortoise and the World|In many Hindu and Native American stories the earth rests on the back of a great turtle. The world is carried by patience and slowness.|Hindu and Lenape myth||e|many cultures|In Hindu tradition, Kurma, the tortoise avatar of Vishnu, supports Mount Mandara during the churning of the ocean, and later traditions place the world on elephants standing on a turtle. Many Native American peoples, including the Lenape and Haudenosaunee, call North America Turtle Island, telling how earth was placed on a turtle's back.
mythworld|Raven Steals the Light|In tales of the Pacific Northwest, the trickster Raven steals the sun, moon and stars from a selfish chief who kept them in boxes, and releases them into the sky for everyone.|Haida and Tlingit stories||e|Pacific Northwest|Among the Haida, Tlingit and other peoples of the Pacific Northwest, Raven is a creator and trickster. In one story he transforms into a pine needle, is swallowed by the chief's daughter, and is reborn as her son. The chief indulges the child, who begs to play with the boxes, then flies off with the light and sets it in the sky.
mythworld|The Rainbow Serpent|In many Aboriginal Australian traditions a great serpent shaped the land as it moved, carving rivers and waterholes. It is a giver of water and life, and dangerous when angered.|Aboriginal Australian traditions||e|Australia|The Rainbow Serpent appears in the traditions of many Aboriginal Australian peoples, with different names and stories, such as Ngalyod and Wagyl. In the Dreaming, the creative time, its movements formed valleys, rivers and waterholes. It is associated with water, fertility and the law of the land, and with the rainbow itself. These are some of the oldest continuous traditions on Earth.
mythworld|Coyote Brings Fire|In many Native American tales, Coyote steals fire from those who hoard it and, with the help of other animals passing it along, brings warmth to the people.|Native American stories||e|North America|Coyote is a trickster in the stories of many peoples of western North America. In one widespread version, fire was guarded by beings on a mountain. Coyote snatched a burning brand, and as the guardians chased him, animals passed it from one to another, squirrel, chipmunk, frog, until it was hidden in wood, from which people can now draw fire by rubbing sticks.
mythworld|Sedna of the Sea|In Inuit myth, Sedna's fingers become the seals, whales and fish when she falls into the sea. Hunters must respect her, or she withholds the animals.|Inuit myth||e|Arctic|In one Inuit version, Sedna refused suitors and married a bird spirit who deceived her. When her father tried to rescue her by boat, a storm struck, and he threw her overboard. As she clung to the kayak, he cut off her fingers, which became sea creatures. Sedna sank to rule the ocean depths, and shamans would visit to comb her hair and calm her.
mythworld|Izanagi and Izanami|The Japanese creator gods stir the ocean with a jewelled spear to make the first island. When Izanami dies, Izanagi follows her to the underworld and, like Orpheus, looks when he should not.|The Kojiki||e|712|In the Kojiki, Izanagi and Izanami stood on the floating bridge of heaven and stirred the sea; drops from the spear formed the first island, where they married and gave birth to the islands and gods of Japan. After Izanami died giving birth to fire, Izanagi went to fetch her from Yomi, but looking at her decaying form broke his promise, and they parted forever.
mythworld|The Monkey King|Sun Wukong, born from a stone, masters magic and storms heaven, but cannot escape the Buddha's palm. His long journey west is about taming a wild, brilliant mind.|Journey to the West||e|16th century|Journey to the West, a 16th century Chinese novel attributed to Wu Cheng'en, follows the monk Xuanzang to India to fetch Buddhist scriptures. His protector, the brilliant and rebellious Sun Wukong, once rampaged through heaven until the Buddha trapped him under a mountain. On the journey he learns loyalty and discipline. The novel is a comic adventure and a spiritual allegory.
mythworld|Gilgamesh and Enkidu|The wild man Enkidu is tamed by friendship with King Gilgamesh, and his death sends Gilgamesh searching for immortality. The oldest epic is, at heart, about grief for a friend.|The Epic of Gilgamesh||e|c. 2100 BC|The gods created Enkidu, a wild man living among animals, to rival the arrogant king Gilgamesh of Uruk. After a fierce wrestling match, they became inseparable friends and shared adventures, killing the monster Humbaba and the Bull of Heaven. When Enkidu died as punishment, Gilgamesh was devastated and terrified of death, beginning his quest.
mythancient|The Weighing of the Heart|In Egyptian belief, the dead stand before Osiris while Anubis weighs their heart against the feather of Maat, truth. A heart heavy with wrongdoing is devoured; a light one passes to paradise.|Egyptian myth||e|ancient Egypt|For ancient Egyptians, the heart held a person's thoughts, memory and character. In the judgement scene, painted in many tombs and papyri, Anubis weighs it against the feather of Maat, goddess of truth and cosmic order, while Thoth records the result. Those found worthy were welcomed by Osiris into the Field of Reeds, a paradise much like Egypt itself.
mythancient|Ra's Night Journey|Each night the sun god Ra sails through the underworld, battling the chaos serpent Apep so the sun can rise again. Every dawn is a victory over darkness.|Egyptian myth||e|ancient Egypt|Egyptians believed the sun god Ra crossed the sky by day in a boat and at night travelled through the Duat, the underworld, in twelve hours of darkness. There the giant serpent Apep tried to swallow him, and gods fought to defend the boat. Each sunrise meant order had triumphed. Temple rituals and texts in royal tombs helped the sun on this journey.
ideaphil|The Ladder of Love|And the true order of going, or being led by another, to the things of love, is to begin from the beauties of earth and mount upwards for the sake of that other beauty, using these as steps only, and from one going on to two, and from two to all fair forms, and from fair forms to fair practices, and from fair practices to fair notions, until from fair notions he arrives at the notion of absolute beauty, and at last knows what the essence of beauty is.|Plato, Symposium (tr. Benjamin Jowett)|Diotima's teaching: love begins with one beautiful person and climbs, step by step, to the love of beauty itself. Desire, rightly followed, leads upward.|q||In Plato's Symposium, guests at a dinner party give speeches in praise of love. Socrates recounts what the priestess Diotima taught him: love leads us from admiring one beautiful body to all beautiful bodies, then to beautiful souls, laws and knowledge, and finally to Beauty itself. The idea of Platonic love comes from this vision of love as a path to wisdom.
ideaphil|The Restless Heart|Thou madest us for Thyself, and our heart is restless, until it repose in Thee.|Augustine, Confessions (tr. E. B. Pusey)|Augustine's opening prayer: no earthly thing can fully satisfy us, because we are made for something infinite. Restlessness is a sign of where we belong.|q||Augustine of Hippo wrote the Confessions around AD 400, an early spiritual autobiography telling of his restless youth, his search through philosophies and pleasures, and his conversion to Christianity. Its opening words express his conviction that human longing points toward God. Many readers, religious or not, recognise the restlessness he describes.
ideaphil|The Wheel of Fortune|Thou hast resigned thyself to the sway of Fortune; thou must submit to thy mistress's caprices. What! art thou verily striving to stay the swing of the revolving wheel? Oh, stupidest of mortals, if it takes to standing still, it ceases to be the wheel of Fortune.|Boethius, The Consolation of Philosophy (tr. H. R. James)|Writing in prison, Boethius hears Philosophy remind him that fortune always turns. Whoever rides the wheel up must accept its turning down; only inner goods are truly ours.|q||Boethius, a Roman statesman, wrote The Consolation of Philosophy around 524 while awaiting execution on false charges. Lady Philosophy appears and reminds him that Fortune is fickle by nature: she gives and takes. True happiness lies in virtue and wisdom, which no one can take away. The book was one of the most widely read works of the Middle Ages.
ideaphil|The Thinking Reed|Man is but a reed, the most feeble thing in nature; but he is a thinking reed. The entire universe need not arm itself to crush him. A vapour, a drop of water suffices to kill him. But, if the universe were to crush him, man would still be more noble than that which killed him.|Blaise Pascal, Pensées (tr. W. F. Trotter)|We are fragile, easily crushed by the universe, yet greater than it, because we know it and know that we die. Our dignity lies in thought.|q||Pascal reflected on humanity's strange position between the infinitely large and the infinitely small. Physically we are nothing compared with the universe; a drop of water can kill us. But we know we die, and the universe knows nothing. This awareness gives us dignity. Let us strive to think well, he wrote, for that is the principle of morality.
ideaphil|It's How We See Things|Men are disturbed not by things, but by the views which they take of things. Thus death is nothing terrible, else it would have appeared so to Socrates. But the terror consists in our notion of death, that it is terrible.|Epictetus, The Enchiridion (tr. Thomas Wentworth Higginson)|Events don't upset us by themselves; our judgements about them do. Change the judgement and the distress changes, the root of Stoic practice and of modern cognitive therapy.|q||This is the fifth section of Epictetus's Enchiridion, or Handbook, compiled by his student Arrian. When we are troubled, Epictetus says, we should blame not others or events but our own opinions. Psychologists Albert Ellis and Aaron Beck, founders of cognitive therapies, credited this insight as an inspiration for helping people change distressing thoughts.
ideaphil|A Foolish Consistency|A foolish consistency is the hobgoblin of little minds, adored by little statesmen and philosophers and divines. With consistency a great soul has simply nothing to do.|Ralph Waldo Emerson, Self-Reliance|Don't let your past words imprison your present mind. Growth means being willing to contradict yourself.|q||Emerson wrote this in Self-Reliance, urging readers not to cling to earlier opinions simply to appear consistent. Speak what you think now in hard words, he said, and tomorrow speak what tomorrow thinks. He wasn't rejecting all consistency, only the foolish kind that stops us from growing, changing our minds or following new insight.
ideaphil|The View from Above|To comprehend the whole world together in thy mind, and the whole course of this present age to represent it unto thyself, and to fix thy thoughts upon the sudden change of every particular object. How short the time is from the generation of anything, unto the dissolution of the same; but how immense and infinite both that which was before the generation, and that which after the generation of it shall be.|Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (tr. Meric Casaubon)|Picture the whole world and the whole of time, and your worries shrink to their true size. The Stoics practised this "view from above" to find calm.|q||Marcus Aurelius, Roman emperor from 161 to 180, wrote his Meditations as private notes to himself, never meant for publication. He often imagined looking down on the world from a great height, seeing human affairs, empires and lives as brief and small. This perspective helped him handle stress, anger and ambition with calm. Astronauts report a similar overview effect.
ideaphil|Be Still a Man|Be a philosopher; but, amidst all your philosophy, be still a man.|David Hume, An Enquiry Concerning Human Understanding|Hume's advice to himself: think deeply, but don't let thinking cut you off from ordinary life, friendship and pleasure.|q||In the opening section of his Enquiry, Hume imagines nature's advice to people tempted to bury themselves in abstract study. He warns against philosophy that cuts us off from life and action. Hume himself enjoyed company, food and games; he wrote that after hours of sceptical thinking, a game of backgammon with friends restored him to ordinary life.
ideaphil|What Do I Know?|This fancy will be more certainly understood by interrogation: “What do I know?” as I bear it with the emblem of a balance.|Michel de Montaigne, Essays (tr. Charles Cotton)|Que sais-je? Montaigne took a question, not an answer, as his motto: an open, humble doubt that keeps us curious and tolerant.|q||Montaigne had this question engraved on a medal with a set of balanced scales, symbolising suspended judgement. In his Essays, he examined himself with honesty and humour, noting contradictions and uncertainties. His scepticism made him tolerant in a time of religious wars, wary of anyone who claims absolute certainty. The essay form itself means an attempt.
ideaphil|The Will to Believe|William James argued that where evidence can't settle a living question, such as faith or trust in others, we may rightly choose to believe, because refusing to choose is also a choice.|William James||e|1896|In his 1896 lecture, William James responded to the claim that it is always wrong to believe without sufficient evidence. Some choices are live, forced and momentous: not deciding is itself a decision. In such cases, he argued, our passions may legitimately decide. Faith in a friend or in our own abilities can even help create the facts that justify it.
ideaphil|Lived Time|Bergson distinguished clock time, cut into equal units, from durée, time as we live it: flowing, stretching and shrinking with our attention and feeling.|Henri Bergson||e|1889|Henri Bergson, a French philosopher, argued in his doctoral thesis that science treats time like space, as a line of separate moments. But real time, durée, is experienced as continuous flow where past and present interpenetrate. An hour of boredom and an hour of joy feel completely different. His ideas influenced Proust, Woolf and modernist literature.
ideaphil|Dare to Know|Sapere aude! Kant's motto for the Enlightenment: have the courage to use your own understanding, without someone else's guidance.|Immanuel Kant||e|1784|Kant's 1784 essay What Is Enlightenment? defined enlightenment as leaving self-imposed immaturity, the inability to think without someone else's guidance. The cause, he said, is not lack of intelligence but lack of courage. The Latin motto, from the poet Horace, became a rallying cry for independent thinking, free inquiry and public debate.
ideaphil|The Master and the Slave|Hegel's parable: the master depends on the slave's recognition, and the slave, through work, comes to know himself. We become selves only through being recognised by others.|G. W. F. Hegel||e|1807|In the Phenomenology of Spirit, Hegel imagined two self-conscious beings struggling for recognition. One submits and becomes a slave; the other becomes master. But the master depends on someone he doesn't respect, while the slave, through labour, shapes the world and gains self-awareness. The passage influenced Marx, existentialism and theories of recognition.
ideaphil|Know Thyself|The words carved at Apollo's temple at Delphi: understand your own nature and limits. Socrates made it the starting point of philosophy.|Delphic maxim||e|ancient Greece|Gnothi seauton was one of the maxims inscribed at the temple of Apollo at Delphi, along with nothing in excess. It originally may have meant know your place as a mortal. Socrates turned it into a call for lifelong self-examination. Psychology, philosophy and spiritual traditions all still treat self-knowledge as the basis of wisdom.
ideaphil|The Social Construction of Reality|Much of what seems natural, money, nations, marriage, roles, exists because we agree it does. Shared belief builds a world that then shapes us.|Peter Berger and Thomas Luckmann||e|1966|Peter Berger and Thomas Luckmann's 1966 book argued that society is a human product, people are social products, and society seems objective because we create it together and then inherit it. Institutions feel like natural facts, but they are habits that became traditions. This insight helps us see that what was made by people can be remade.
ideasci|Kepler's Laws|Planets move in ellipses, not perfect circles, sweeping out equal areas in equal times. Kepler gave up a beautiful theory to follow the data.|Johannes Kepler||e|1609–1619|Johannes Kepler, a German astronomer, worked with Tycho Brahe's precise observations of Mars. Believing planets must move in circles, he struggled for years with a stubborn error of eight arcminutes, then accepted that orbits are ellipses. His three laws describe how planets move, and Newton later explained them with gravity. It is a model of letting evidence overrule preference.
ideasci|The Conservation of Mass|In a chemical reaction, nothing is created or lost; matter only changes form. Lavoisier's careful weighing founded modern chemistry.|Antoine Lavoisier||e|1789|Antoine Lavoisier, a French chemist, carefully weighed substances before and after reactions in sealed containers and showed that total mass stays the same. He named oxygen and hydrogen and disproved the phlogiston theory of burning. His Elementary Treatise on Chemistry of 1789 founded modern chemistry. He was guillotined in the French Revolution in 1794.
ideasci|Fields|Faraday imagined invisible lines of force filling space around magnets and currents. The idea of a field, not action at a distance, now underlies all of physics.|Michael Faraday||e|1830s–1850s|Michael Faraday, the son of a blacksmith with little formal schooling, became one of the greatest experimental scientists. He discovered electromagnetic induction, the basis of electric generators. Rejecting action at a distance, he imagined lines of force filling space. James Clerk Maxwell put the idea into equations, and fields became central to all modern physics.
ideasci|Plate Tectonics|The Earth's surface is broken into moving plates. Where they collide, mountains rise and earthquakes strike; where they part, new ocean floor is born.|many geologists||e|1960s|In the 1960s, mapping of the ocean floor revealed mid-ocean ridges where new crust forms, and magnetic stripes recording reversals of Earth's magnetic field. This confirmed Wegener's continental drift and explained it: Earth's outer shell is broken into about a dozen large plates moving over the mantle. Plate tectonics explains earthquakes, volcanoes and mountain ranges.
ideasci|The Microbiome|Each of us carries trillions of microbes, about as many cells as our own, that help digest food, train the immune system and even affect mood.|many researchers||e|2000s|The Human Microbiome Project, begun in 2007, mapped the bacteria, viruses and fungi living in and on us. Gut microbes help digest fibre, make vitamins and influence immunity. Research suggests links to conditions from allergies to obesity and possibly mental health, though much is still being understood. Diet, antibiotics and environment shape this inner ecosystem.
ideasci|Epigenetics|Genes can be switched on or off by experience and environment without changing the DNA itself. What we are born with is not the whole story.|Conrad Waddington and others||e|1942 onward|Conrad Waddington, a British biologist, coined the term in 1942 to describe how genes interact with environment to produce traits. He pictured development as a ball rolling down a landscape of valleys. Today epigenetics studies chemical tags on DNA that switch genes on or off, influenced by diet, stress and experience, sometimes across generations.
ideasci|The Goldilocks Zone|A planet can hold liquid water only at the right distance from its star, not too hot and not too cold. Life as we know it needs that narrow band.|Hubertus Strughold and others||e|1950s|The habitable zone, or Goldilocks zone, named after the fairy tale's porridge that was neither too hot nor too cold, is the region around a star where a planet could have liquid water on its surface. Earth sits comfortably in the Sun's. Astronomers now search exoplanets in the habitable zones of other stars for signs of life.
ideasci|Spacetime Curves|Mass bends space and time, and that bending is what we feel as gravity. Light passing the Sun is deflected, as Eddington's eclipse photographs showed in 1919.|Albert Einstein||e|1915|Einstein's general theory of relativity, completed in 1915, describes gravity as the curvature of spacetime by mass and energy. As the physicist John Wheeler summarised: matter tells space how to curve; space tells matter how to move. Its predictions, from bending light to gravitational waves and black holes, have been confirmed again and again.
ideamind|The Default Mode|When we stop focusing on a task, a network of brain regions becomes active that daydreams, remembers and imagines the future. Mind-wandering has its own purpose.|Marcus Raichle||e|2001|Neuroscientist Marcus Raichle noticed that certain brain areas became more active when people were resting between tasks in scans. This default mode network is involved in self-reflection, memory, imagining the future and thinking about others. Excessive activity may relate to rumination and depression, while meditation seems to quiet it. Daydreaming can also aid creativity.
ideamind|Mere Exposure|We tend to like things simply because we've seen them before. Familiarity breeds not contempt, but comfort.|Robert Zajonc||e|1968|Robert Zajonc showed people unfamiliar words, shapes and faces. The more often they had seen an item, the more they liked it, even when they didn't remember seeing it. Advertisers rely on repetition for this reason. The effect has limits, as too much exposure can lead to boredom, but it explains why new songs grow on us.
ideamind|The Halo Effect|One good trait, such as beauty or confidence, makes us assume others: kindness, intelligence, honesty. First impressions colour everything.|Edward Thorndike||e|1920|Edward Thorndike, an American psychologist, found that military officers who rated a soldier highly in one quality tended to rate him highly in others. Attractive people are often judged smarter or kinder. The effect influences hiring, grading and courtrooms. Its opposite, the horns effect, lets one bad trait cast a shadow over everything else.
ideamind|Self-Fulfilling Prophecy|Expecting something can make it happen: rumour of a bank failing causes a run on the bank, and believing in a pupil helps the pupil grow.|Robert K. Merton||e|1948|Sociologist Robert K. Merton coined the term in 1948. A false belief can make itself true by changing behaviour: rumours of a bank's collapse lead customers to withdraw money, causing the collapse. In education, teachers' expectations can influence students' performance. Positive expectations can work the same way, which is a reason to expect the best of people.
ideamind|Gratitude|Regularly noticing what is good in our lives measurably lifts mood and wellbeing. Attention trained on blessings finds more of them.|Robert Emmons and Michael McCullough||e|2003|In a 2003 study, Robert Emmons and Michael McCullough asked people to write weekly lists of things they were grateful for, hassles, or neutral events. The gratitude group felt more optimistic, exercised more and reported fewer physical complaints. Gratitude practices are now widely used in wellbeing programmes. Small, specific notes of appreciation seem to work best.
ideamind|Post-Traumatic Growth|Many people who live through great suffering report deeper relationships, new priorities and a stronger sense of meaning afterwards. Wounds can also be openings.|Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun||e|1996|Psychologists Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun studied people who had faced bereavement, illness and disaster. Alongside pain, many described greater appreciation of life, closer relationships, new possibilities and personal strength. Growth doesn't mean the trauma was good or that suffering disappears, and no one should be pressured to find a silver lining.
ideasoc|The Commons Can Be Governed|Ostrom showed that communities around the world manage shared fish, forests and water well for centuries, through their own rules, without either privatisation or state control.|Elinor Ostrom||e|1990|Elinor Ostrom, the first woman to win the Nobel Prize in economics, studied Swiss alpine meadows, Japanese forests, Spanish irrigation systems and fisheries. She found that communities often avoid the tragedy of the commons by developing clear rules, monitoring, graduated sanctions and ways to resolve conflict. Her work offers hope for managing shared resources today.
ideasoc|Cultural Hegemony|Power rules not only by force but by shaping what seems normal and common sense, so that people consent to arrangements that don't serve them.|Antonio Gramsci||e|1930s|Antonio Gramsci, an Italian Marxist imprisoned by Mussolini's regime, wrote his Prison Notebooks in the 1930s. He asked why workers didn't revolt and concluded that ruling groups gain consent by shaping culture, education and media, making their worldview seem natural. Changing society, he argued, requires changing ideas and culture as well as institutions.
ideasoc|Civil Disobedience|When a law is unjust, Thoreau argued, a person of conscience should refuse to obey it and accept the penalty. Gandhi and King made the idea a force for change.|Henry David Thoreau||e|1849|Thoreau spent a night in jail in 1846 for refusing to pay a poll tax that supported slavery and the Mexican-American War. In his essay he argued that individuals should not let governments override their conscience. His ideas inspired Tolstoy, Gandhi's independence movement in India, and Martin Luther King Jr.'s civil rights movement.
ideasoc|The Gift|In many societies, gifts create bonds: to receive obliges one to give in return. Exchange of gifts weaves relationships that markets alone cannot.|Marcel Mauss||e|1925|Marcel Mauss, a French anthropologist, studied gift exchanges like the potlatch of Pacific Northwest peoples and the kula ring of the Trobriand Islands. He found gifts are never entirely free: they create obligations to receive and to give back, binding people together. His essay influenced anthropology, sociology and thinking about generosity and community.
ideamath|The Prisoner and the Infinite Hotel|A hotel with infinitely many rooms, all full, can still take a new guest: everyone moves up one room. Infinity behaves unlike any finite number.|David Hilbert||e|1924|The German mathematician David Hilbert described this thought experiment in a 1924 lecture to illustrate Cantor's infinite sets. A full hotel with infinitely many rooms can even accommodate infinitely many new guests, by moving each current guest to the room with double their number. The playful puzzle shows how infinity defies everyday intuition.
ideamath|Proof by Contradiction|To prove something, assume it is false and show that this leads to absurdity. Euclid used it to show there are infinitely many primes.|Greek mathematics||e|antiquity|Known in Latin as reductio ad absurdum, this method assumes the opposite of what you want to prove, then shows that leads to an impossibility. Ancient Greek mathematicians used it to prove that the square root of two is not a fraction. It remains a powerful tool in mathematics and logic, and in everyday argument.
ideamath|Symmetry|Many of nature's deepest laws come from symmetry: Noether proved that every continuous symmetry of physics corresponds to something conserved, like energy.|Emmy Noether||e|1918|Emmy Noether, a German mathematician, proved her theorem in 1918. If the laws of physics stay the same over time, energy is conserved; if they are the same everywhere in space, momentum is conserved. Albert Einstein praised her as a creative mathematical genius. Her theorem is fundamental to modern physics, even though she faced discrimination as a woman.
ideamath|Chaos Is Not Randomness|Simple, fully determined equations can produce behaviour that looks random and can never be predicted far ahead. Order and unpredictability live together.|Edward Lorenz and others||e|1960s–1970s|Chaos theory showed that deterministic systems, governed by exact rules with no randomness, can behave unpredictably because of sensitivity to initial conditions. Examples include weather, dripping taps and some planetary orbits. Within the chaos there are often hidden structures called strange attractors. Chaos science changed how we think about prediction and complexity.
ideaeast|The Ox-Herding Pictures|Ten Zen pictures trace the path to awakening as a herder searching for, taming and riding home an ox, then forgetting both, and finally returning to the marketplace with open hands.|Kuòān Shīyuǎn||e|12th century|The Chinese Chan master Kuoan Shiyuan created the best-known version of these ten images and verses in the 12th century. The ox represents our true nature or mind. The series moves from seeking and taming to an empty circle, representing emptiness, then the return to nature, and finally entering the town to help others. Enlightenment ends in everyday compassion.
ideaeast|Mindfulness|Paying deliberate, gentle attention to the present moment, to breath, body and thought, without judging it. The Buddha's practice, now widely taught for everyday life.|The Buddha||e|5th century BC|Sati, the Pali word translated as mindfulness, is the seventh factor of the Eightfold Path. Buddhist texts describe it as attending to body, feelings, mind and phenomena. Since the 1970s, Jon Kabat-Zinn and others have adapted mindfulness into secular programmes, now used in medicine, schools and workplaces to reduce stress and improve focus and wellbeing.
ideaeast|Ren|Confucius's highest virtue, often translated humaneness: the warm, considerate way of being human with others, learned in family and extended to all.|Confucius||e|5th century BC|Ren, written with characters for person and two, describes the virtue that arises between people. Confucius taught that it begins with filial respect and family love and extends outward to friends, community and all humanity. Practising ren means treating others with empathy, courtesy and good faith. Confucius said that anyone who truly desires ren will find it near.
ideaeast|Ahimsa|Non-harm toward all living beings, central to Jain, Hindu and Buddhist ethics. Gandhi made it the root of nonviolent resistance.|Jain, Hindu and Buddhist teaching||e|ancient India|Ahimsa, not harming, is the first principle of Jain ethics, which extends it even to insects and microorganisms. In Hinduism and Buddhism it underlies vegetarianism and compassion. Mahatma Gandhi used ahimsa as the foundation of satyagraha, nonviolent resistance against British rule, and influenced civil rights movements worldwide.
ideaart|Ekphrasis|Writing that brings a work of visual art to life in words, from Homer's shield of Achilles to Keats's Grecian urn.|Greek rhetoric||e|antiquity|Ekphrasis, Greek for description, was originally a rhetorical exercise in vivid description. In literature it refers to poems or passages about artworks. Famous examples include Homer's long description of the shield of Achilles, Keats's Ode on a Grecian Urn and W. H. Auden's Musée des Beaux Arts. It explores the relationship between words and images.
ideaart|The Unreliable Narrator|A storyteller we can't fully trust, through bias, ignorance or deceit. Reading becomes detective work, and truth something we assemble ourselves.|Wayne C. Booth||e|1961|The American literary critic Wayne C. Booth coined the term in The Rhetoric of Fiction. Unreliable narrators may lie, misunderstand or be self-deceived, like the governess in The Turn of the Screw or the butler Stevens in The Remains of the Day. They make readers question what is true and reflect on how all perspectives are limited.
ideaart|The Iceberg Theory|Write only the tip and let the reader feel the rest beneath the surface. What is left out, Hemingway said, strengthens what remains.|Ernest Hemingway||e|1932|In Death in the Afternoon, Hemingway wrote that the dignity of movement of an iceberg is due to only one-eighth of it being above water. A writer who truly knows the subject can omit things, and the reader will feel them as strongly as if stated. His spare short stories, like Hills Like White Elephants, leave the central matter unspoken.
mythgreek|Like the Leaves|Men come and go as leaves year by year upon the trees. Those of autumn the wind sheds upon the ground, but when spring returns the forest buds forth with fresh vines. Even so is it with the generations of mankind, the new spring up as the old are passing away.|Homer, The Iliad, Book VI (tr. Samuel Butler)|On the battlefield, a warrior answers a question about his ancestry by reminding his enemy how briefly any of us lasts. Generations fall and return like leaves.|q||In Book VI of the Iliad, the Greek hero Diomedes asks the Trojan ally Glaucus who he is. Glaucus answers that human generations are like leaves, falling and returning with each season, before telling his family history. The two discover their grandfathers were friends, exchange armour and agree not to fight each other, a rare moment of humanity in war.
mythgreek|Hector and His Son|He stretched his arms towards his child, but the boy cried and nestled in his nurse’s bosom, scared at the sight of his father’s armour, and at the horse-hair plume that nodded fiercely from his helmet. His father and mother laughed to see him, but Hector took the helmet from his head and laid it all gleaming upon the ground.|Homer, The Iliad, Book VI (tr. Samuel Butler)|The great warrior's baby is frightened by his helmet, so Hector takes it off and laughs with his wife. A moment of tenderness before a war that will take everything.|q||Hector, Troy's greatest defender, visits his wife Andromache and baby son Astyanax before returning to battle. Frightened by the plume of his father's helmet, the child cries, and both parents laugh. Hector prays his son will grow up to be greater than he is. Readers know Troy will fall and Astyanax will die, which makes the scene heartbreaking.
mythgreek|Priam and Achilles|I have steeled myself as no man yet has ever steeled himself before me, and have raised to my lips the hand of him who slew my son. Thus spoke Priam, and the heart of Achilles yearned as he bethought him of his father. He took the old man’s hand and moved him gently away. The two wept bitterly—Priam, as he lay at Achilles’ feet, weeping for Hector, and Achilles now for his father and now for Patroclus, till the house was filled with their lamentation.|Homer, The Iliad, Book XXIV (tr. Samuel Butler)|The old king kisses the hands of the man who killed his son, and the two enemies weep together. The Iliad ends not in victory but in shared grief.|q||After Achilles killed Hector and dragged his body around Troy, King Priam secretly crossed enemy lines to beg for his son's body. Achilles, reminded of his own father, weeps with him. They share a meal and Achilles grants a truce for the funeral. The Iliad, a poem of rage, ends with compassion between enemies.
mythgreek|The Lotus-Eaters|They started at once, and went about among the Lotus-eaters, who did them no hurt, but gave them to eat of the lotus, which was so delicious that those who ate of it left off caring about home, and did not even want to go back and say what had happened to them, but were for staying and munching lotus with the Lotus-eaters without thinking further of their return.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book IX (tr. Samuel Butler)|A sweet flower makes Odysseus's men forget their homes and their purpose. The oldest image of pleasant oblivion, and of how easily we can lose our way.|q||On their journey home from Troy, Odysseus's men landed among the Lotus-eaters, whose sweet fruit made them lose all desire to return home. Odysseus dragged them weeping back to the ships and tied them down. Tennyson's poem The Lotos-Eaters explores the temptation to give up striving. The image fits addictions and distractions that make us forget our goals.
mythgreek|Noman|Cyclops, you ask my name and I will tell it you; give me, therefore, the present you promised me; my name is Noman; this is what my father and mother and my friends have always called me.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book IX (tr. Samuel Butler)|Odysseus tells the Cyclops his name is Nobody, so when the blinded giant cries for help, his neighbours hear that "Nobody" is hurting him. Wit defeats brute strength.|q||Trapped in the cave of the one-eyed giant Polyphemus, who ate several of his men, Odysseus got him drunk and told him his name was Noman, or Nobody. He then blinded the Cyclops with a heated stake. When other Cyclopes asked who was hurting him, Polyphemus shouted Nobody, so they left. Odysseus's later boast of his true name brought Poseidon's anger.
mythgreek|Calypso's Offer|But if you could only know how much suffering is in store for you before you get back to your own country, you would stay where you are, keep house along with me, and let me make you immortal, no matter how anxious you may be to see this wife of yours, of whom you are thinking all the time day after day; yet I flatter myself that I am no whit less tall or well-looking than she is, for it is not to be expected that a mortal woman should compare in beauty with an immortal.|Homer, The Odyssey, Book V (tr. Samuel Butler)|The goddess offers Odysseus eternal life; he chooses his ageing wife and his rocky island. The Odyssey's quiet claim: a mortal, human life is worth more than immortality.|q||The nymph Calypso held Odysseus on her island for seven years. When the gods ordered his release, she offered him immortality if he would stay. He admitted she was more beautiful than his wife Penelope, yet longed for home. He chose mortality, ageing and family over endless life, affirming the value of an ordinary human existence.
mythgreek|Atlas|And Atlas through hard constraint upholds the wide heaven with unwearying head and arms, standing at the borders of the earth before the clear-voiced Hesperides; for this lot wise Zeus assigned to him.|Hesiod, Theogony (tr. Hugh G. Evelyn-White)|Punished for fighting the gods, the Titan Atlas holds up the sky forever. He has become the image of anyone carrying a weight too great for one person.|q||Atlas was a Titan who fought against Zeus and the Olympian gods. As punishment, he was condemned to hold up the sky at the western edge of the world. In one story Heracles briefly took the burden while Atlas fetched golden apples. A book of maps was named an atlas after a 16th century image of him holding up the heavens.
mythgreek|Prometheus Bound|And ready-witted Prometheus he bound with inextricable bonds, cruel chains, and drove a shaft through his middle, and set on him a long-winged eagle, which used to eat his immortal liver; but by night the liver grew as much again everyway as the long-winged bird devoured in the whole day.|Hesiod, Theogony (tr. Hugh G. Evelyn-White)|For giving humans fire, Prometheus is chained and an eagle eats his liver each day, and it grows back each night. Defiance for the sake of others has a price that renews itself.|q||Hesiod tells how Zeus bound Prometheus in chains and sent an eagle to devour his immortal liver, which regrew each night, a torment without end. Later Heracles killed the eagle and freed him. The tragedy Prometheus Bound, attributed to Aeschylus, presents him as a noble rebel, and Percy Shelley's Prometheus Unbound made him a Romantic hero.
mythgreek|Aurora and Tithonus|Her greatest favorite was Tithonus, son of Laomedon, king of Troy. She stole him away, and prevailed on Jupiter to grant him immortality; but, forgetting to have youth joined in the gift, after some time she began to discern, to her great mortification, that he was growing old.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|The Dawn wins immortality for her mortal lover but forgets to ask for youth. He grows older forever. Be careful what you ask for: endless life without youth is no gift.|q||Eos, called Aurora by the Romans, goddess of the dawn, fell in love with the Trojan prince Tithonus. She asked Zeus to make him immortal but forgot to ask for eternal youth. As he aged and withered, she shut him in a room, where he babbled endlessly; in some versions he became a cicada. Tennyson's poem Tithonus explores the curse of endless age.
mythgreek|Endymion|Endymion was a beautiful youth who fed his flock on Mount Latmos. One calm, clear night Diana, the moon, looked down and saw him sleeping. The cold heart of the virgin goddess was warmed by his surpassing beauty, and she came down to him, kissed him, and watched over him while he slept.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|The Moon falls in love with a sleeping shepherd and visits him every night. Keats made the story a hymn to beauty: "A thing of beauty is a joy for ever."|q||Endymion, a handsome shepherd, was loved by the moon goddess Selene, or Diana. In some versions she asked Zeus to grant him eternal sleep so he could stay young forever, and she visited him nightly. John Keats's long poem Endymion, published in 1818, begins with the famous line about beauty and its lasting joy.
mythgreek|Hero and Leander|Leander loved her, and used to swim the strait nightly to enjoy the company of his mistress, guided by a torch which she reared upon the tower for the purpose. But one night a tempest arose and the sea was rough; his strength failed, and he was drowned.|Thomas Bulfinch, The Age of Fable|Every night Leander swims the Hellespont, guided by Hero's lamp, until a storm puts out the light. Lord Byron swam the strait himself to prove it could be done.|q||Hero was a priestess of Aphrodite at Sestos, and Leander lived across the Hellespont strait at Abydos. Every night he swam to her by the light of her lamp. One stormy night the wind blew out the light, and Leander drowned; Hero threw herself from her tower in grief. In 1810 Lord Byron swam the Hellespont, about four miles, to prove it possible.
mythnorse|Man Is Man's Delight|Young was I once, and wandered alone,\nAnd nought of the road I knew;\nRich did I feel when a comrade I found,\nFor man is man’s delight.|The Poetic Edda, Hovamol (tr. Companionship is the true wealth.|q||The Havamal, sayings of the High One, gathers practical wisdom attributed to Odin about hospitality, moderation and friendship. In a harsh Northern world, being alone could mean danger and despair. These lines suggest that friendship makes us rich more than gold. Other verses advise visiting friends often, since weeds grow on paths rarely walked.
mythnorse|Fenrir and Tyr's Hand|The gods could only bind the giant wolf Fenrir if one of them put a hand in his mouth as a pledge. Tyr, god of law, did, and lost it. Keeping order sometimes costs a sacrifice no one else will make.|The Prose Edda||e|13th century|Fenrir, a monstrous wolf born to Loki, grew so strong that the gods feared him. Tyr volunteered, and when Fenrir couldn't break free, he bit it off.
mythnorse|Bifröst|The gods built a burning rainbow bridge between Asgard and the world of humans.
