When the giant Thjazi steals her away, the gods begin to grow old and grey, until Loki is forced to bring her back.|The Prose Edda||e|13th century|Loki, captured by Thjazi in eagle form, bought his freedom by luring Idun out of Asgard. Ageing quickly, the gods threatened Loki, who turned into a falcon, transformed Idun into a nut and flew her home. The pursuing Thjazi was burned by fires on Asgard's walls. The myth shows even gods depend on care for what keeps them alive.
mythworld|Rama's Promise|Prince Rama accepts fourteen years of exile in the forest so that his father can keep a promise. Duty and honour matter more to him than a throne.|The Ramayana||e|c. 5th century BC – 1st century AD|The Ramayana, attributed to the sage Valmiki, tells how King Dasharatha, bound by a promise to his queen Kaikeyi, had to banish his beloved son Rama. Rama went willingly, followed by his wife Sita and brother Lakshmana. He is honoured as the ideal of dharma, righteous duty. The festival of Diwali celebrates his return home.
mythworld|Hanuman's Leap|When Sita is carried off to the island of Lanka, the monkey Hanuman remembers his forgotten strength and leaps across the ocean to find her. Sometimes we need reminding of what we are capable of.|The Ramayana||e|c. 5th century BC – 1st century AD|Hanuman, son of the wind god, had been cursed as a child to forget his powers until someone reminded him. When the monkey army stood helpless at the shore, the bear king Jambavan told him of his strength. Hanuman grew enormous and leapt to Lanka. He is beloved across India and Southeast Asia as the image of devotion, courage and service.
mythworld|Sita in the Fire|After Rama defeats the demon king Ravana, Sita walks into fire to prove her faithfulness, and the fire god returns her unharmed. Later tellers have asked why she had to be tested at all.|The Ramayana||e|c. 5th century BC – 1st century AD|Sita, kidnapped by Ravana and held in Lanka, refused him throughout. Her trial by fire, the agni pariksha, is one of the epic's most debated scenes. Some later versions have her finally return to the earth, her mother, rather than face more doubt. Many modern readers see in Sita's story a powerful questioning of the burdens placed on women.
ideamind|Linguistic Relativity|The language we speak shapes, at least a little, how we notice and remember the world: colours, directions, time. Strong versions are disputed; gentle versions are well supported.|Edward Sapir and Benjamin Lee Whorf||e|1920s–1940s|Benjamin Lee Whorf, a fire-insurance inspector and linguist, claimed the Hopi language had a different sense of time, which later research disputed. But studies have found real effects: speakers of Guugu Yimithirr in Australia, who use compass directions instead of left and right, stay oriented remarkably well, and Russian speakers, with separate words for light and dark blue, distinguish those shades faster.
ideamind|Universal Grammar|Noam Chomsky argued that children learn language so fast, from so little, because they are born with a deep grammar shared by all human languages. Each language is a variation on one plan.|Noam Chomsky||e|1957–1965|Chomsky, an American linguist, transformed linguistics with Syntactic Structures in 1957. He pointed out that children produce sentences they have never heard and acquire complex rules without being taught. His theory sparked decades of debate: some researchers argue general learning and social interaction explain language better. The question of what is inborn remains open.
ideamind|Metaphors We Live By|We think in metaphors without noticing: argument is war ("she attacked my point"), time is money ("spend an hour"), happy is up ("feeling low"). Change the metaphor and you change how you see.|George Lakoff and Mark Johnson||e|1980|The linguist George Lakoff and philosopher Mark Johnson argued that metaphor is not decoration but the basis of everyday thought, rooted in bodily experience. Thinking of an argument as a dance rather than a war would change how we argue. Their ideas influenced politics, where framing issues through metaphor shapes how people understand them.
ideamind|Lost in a Story|When we are absorbed in a story, transported into its world, our attitudes shift toward the story's view, and we argue back less. Stories persuade by inviting us in, not by proving.|Melanie Green and Timothy Brock||e|2000|Psychologists Melanie Green and Timothy Brock measured transportation, how deeply readers were absorbed by a narrative. More transported readers adopted beliefs consistent with the story, even when told it was fiction. This explains the power of novels, films and testimonials, and why stories can foster empathy, or spread misinformation.
ideamind|Gossip as Grooming|Robin Dunbar argued that language evolved partly to let us do with words what other primates do by grooming: bond, keep track of friends and learn whom to trust. Most of our talk is about other people.|Robin Dunbar||e|1996|Primates spend hours grooming each other to keep alliances, but there's only so much time. As human groups grew larger, Dunbar suggested, talk let us maintain many relationships at once. Studies find around two-thirds of conversation concerns social topics. Gossip is not always idle: it spreads information about reputations and helps enforce fairness.
ideamind|Words Shape Memory|After watching a car crash on film, people asked how fast the cars were going when they "smashed" into each other gave higher speeds, and some later remembered broken glass that wasn't there.|Elizabeth Loftus and John Palmer||e|1974|Elizabeth Loftus, an American psychologist, showed that memory is reconstructive: each time we recall something, we rebuild it, and suggestions can alter it. Her research has reshaped how police interview witnesses and how courts treat testimony. It also shows that the questions we ask ourselves about the past can change how we remember it.
ideamind|The Framing Effect|The same choice feels different depending on how it is described: a treatment that saves 200 of 600 people sounds better than one where 400 die, though they are identical.|Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman||e|1981|In the famous Asian disease problem, Tversky and Kahneman presented the same options framed as lives saved or lives lost. People chose safe options for gains and gambled to avoid losses. Framing influences decisions in medicine, marketing, politics and personal life. Restating a choice both ways helps reveal what it really involves.
ideaphil|Words That Do Things|Some sentences don't describe the world but change it: "I promise", "I apologise", "I name this ship". J. L. Austin called them performatives: to say them, in the right setting, is to act.|J. L. Austin||e|1955|The Oxford philosopher J. L. Austin gave his William James lectures at Harvard in 1955, published as How to Do Things with Words. He noticed that utterances like "I do" at a wedding can succeed or fail depending on circumstances. His speech act theory influenced linguistics, law and the study of how language creates social reality.
ideaphil|The Bundle of Perceptions|For my part, when I enter most intimately into what I call myself, I always stumble on some particular perception or other, of heat or cold, light or shade, love or hatred, pain or pleasure. I never can catch myself at any time without a perception, and never can observe any thing but the perception.|David Hume, A Treatise of Human Nature, Book I (1739)|Look inward for your self and you find only feelings, sights and thoughts, never a separate thing that has them. Hume concluded that the self is a bundle of perceptions in constant flow.|q|1739|Hume tried a simple experiment: look inward and find the self. Each time, he found a particular feeling or sensation, never a separate thing that was having them. He concluded that the self is "a bundle or collection of different perceptions", like a theatre whose scenes are all there is. The idea is strikingly close to the Buddhist teaching of non-self, and it still frames debates about personal identity and consciousness.
ideaphil|The Over-Soul|We live in succession, in division, in parts, in particles. Meantime within man is the soul of the whole; the wise silence; the universal beauty, to which every part and particle is equally related; the eternal One.|Ralph Waldo Emerson, The Over-Soul (1841)|We experience life in pieces, but Emerson believed each of us also shares in one universal mind. In silence and insight we touch what connects us to everything.|q|1841|In his essay "The Over-Soul" Emerson described a universal spirit shared by every person and present in all things. Ordinary life comes to us in fragments, but in moments of insight, in nature, conversation or silence, we sense the whole we belong to. The idea, which draws on Plato and the Upanishads, became central to American Transcendentalism and to Emerson's trust in each person's inner voice.
ideaphil|The Problem of Evil|If God is all-good and all-powerful, why is there suffering? Either God cannot prevent it, or will not, or is not what we thought.|Epicurus (as reported by Lactantius)||e|3rd century BC|The argument is traditionally credited to Epicurus, though it survives only in a summary by the Christian writer Lactantius centuries later, and Hume restated it in his "Dialogues". Thinkers have answered it in many ways: suffering as the price of free will, as a school for the soul, or as a mystery beyond us. Few philosophical questions have been so personal, since most people ask it first not in a seminar but in grief.
ideaphil|The Euthyphro Dilemma|Is something good because the gods love it, or do the gods love it because it is good? Either answer raises hard questions.|Plato, Euthyphro||e|around 399 BC|On his way to trial, Socrates meets Euthyphro, who is prosecuting his own father and is certain he knows what piety is. Socrates' question shows the problem: if goodness is whatever the gods command, it seems arbitrary; if the gods command it because it is good, goodness exists independently of them. The dilemma still shapes debates about the foundations of morality.
ideaphil|Ataraxia|The happiest life is not one of wild pleasures but of tranquillity: freedom from fear, simple needs met, and good friends.|Epicurus||e|around 300 BC|Epicurus has been misread as a lover of luxury, but he lived on bread, water and cheese in a garden with friends. He taught that pleasure is the absence of pain in the body and of trouble in the mind, ataraxia. Fear of death and the gods, and endless desires for wealth and fame, were the great disturbers. Friendship, he said, is the greatest of all means to a happy life.
ideaphil|Suspending Judgement|When the arguments on both sides seem equally strong, hold back from deciding, and peace of mind may follow, "as a shadow follows a body".|Pyrrho and Sextus Empiricus||e|4th century BC – 2nd century AD|Pyrrho, who travelled to India with Alexander the Great, founded an ancient school of scepticism. Sextus Empiricus later described how sceptics set every argument against an equal counter-argument and stopped insisting on either. He compared it to the painter Apelles, who threw his sponge at a picture in frustration and accidentally created the effect he wanted. Doubt, practised gently, can bring calm rather than anxiety.
ideaphil|The Swerve|If atoms only fell in straight lines, nothing would ever meet. A tiny, unpredictable swerve lets them collide, creating worlds and making room for freedom.|Lucretius||e|1st century BC|In his poem "On the Nature of Things", Lucretius set out Epicurus' view that everything is made of atoms moving through the void. To explain both how things form and how we can choose freely, he added the clinamen, a slight swerve at no fixed time or place. The rediscovery of his poem in 1417 helped spark modern science and the idea of a natural universe without supernatural control.
ideaphil|Memento Mori|"Remember that you will die." Keeping death in mind isn't morbid; it clarifies what matters and makes each day precious.|Stoic and Christian tradition||e|antiquity – present|A Roman legend tells of a slave standing behind a triumphant general reminding him that he was only mortal. Stoics like Seneca and Marcus Aurelius urged readers to live each day as if it were complete. Medieval and Renaissance art placed skulls among flowers and books. The aim is not fear but gratitude and focus: knowing time is limited, we stop postponing life.
ideaphil|Rehearsing Misfortune|Imagine, calmly, losing what you have. You'll be less shaken if it happens, and more grateful while it lasts.|Seneca and the Stoics||e|1st century|The Stoics practised praemeditatio malorum, the premeditation of evils. Seneca advised setting aside days to live on little, to discover that poverty is less terrible than we fear. Epictetus suggested reminding yourself, while kissing a child, that they are mortal. Psychologists find related benefits in "negative visualisation": it reduces anxiety and renews appreciation of the ordinary.
ideaphil|God or Nature|God is not a person outside the world but the one infinite substance of which everything is a part. Understanding nature's order is the path to freedom and joy.|Baruch Spinoza||e|1677|Spinoza, expelled from Amsterdam's Jewish community at 23, worked as a lens grinder and wrote his "Ethics" in the form of geometry. He identified God with Nature, "Deus sive Natura", and argued that understanding why things happen frees us from being tossed by passions. Einstein said he believed in "Spinoza's God, who reveals himself in the harmony of all that exists".
ideaphil|Phenomena and Things-in-Themselves|We never see the world as it is in itself, only as it appears through the structures of our minds, such as space, time and cause.|Immanuel Kant||e|1781|Kant's "Critique of Pure Reason" argued that the mind is not a passive mirror. Like tinted glasses we can never take off, it shapes all experience in space and time and links events by cause and effect. What lies beyond, the "thing-in-itself", remains unknowable. This "Copernican revolution" in philosophy shaped nearly all later thought about knowledge and reality.
ideaphil|The Owl of Minerva|Wisdom comes late. We understand an age fully only as it is ending, the way the owl of wisdom takes flight at dusk.|G. W. F. Hegel||e|1820|In the preface to his "Philosophy of Right", Hegel wrote that philosophy always arrives too late to tell the world how it ought to be: "the owl of Minerva spreads its wings only with the falling of the dusk". Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom. The image applies to lives as well as history: we often grasp the meaning of a period only once it is over.
ideaphil|Alienation|When work is something done only for someone else, we become strangers to what we make, to each other and to ourselves.|Karl Marx||e|1844|In notebooks written at 26, Marx described how industrial workers lose control over their products, over the activity of work itself, over their creative human nature and over their bonds with others. Work, which could be an expression of who we are, becomes a mere means to survive. The idea influenced psychology and sociology far beyond Marxism and still names the feeling of meaningless work.
ideaphil|The Dizziness of Freedom|Anxiety is what we feel when we glimpse that we are free to choose, and so responsible for who we become.|Søren Kierkegaard||e|1844|In "The Concept of Anxiety" Kierkegaard compared anxiety to the dizziness of someone looking down into a yawning abyss. It is not fear of any particular thing but the vertigo of possibility. He believed that facing it honestly, rather than fleeing into the crowd, is how we become a true self. The idea shaped existentialism and modern psychology.
ideaphil|Language Games|The meaning of a word lies in how it's used in the activities of life, not in some object it names. To understand a word, look at the game it is part of.|Ludwig Wittgenstein||e|1953|In his later work, "Philosophical Investigations", Wittgenstein rejected his own early view that language pictures the world. Words are like tools in a toolbox, used in countless "language games": giving orders, telling jokes, praying, greeting. Many philosophical confusions, he thought, come from taking words out of the life that gives them meaning. Philosophy should untangle such knots, "to show the fly the way out of the fly-bottle".
ideaphil|What Is It Like to Be a Bat?|A bat experiences the world through echoes we can describe but never feel. Science can tell us how brains work, but can it capture what experience is like from inside?|Thomas Nagel||e|1974|Nagel's famous essay argued that a creature is conscious if there is "something it is like" to be it. We can learn everything about bat sonar and still not know what it feels like. This subjective character of experience seems to resist physical explanation, and the essay helped launch modern debates about what David Chalmers later called the hard problem of consciousness.
ideaphil|The Hard Problem of Consciousness|We can explain how the brain processes colour or pain. But why is all that processing accompanied by an inner experience at all?|David Chalmers||e|1995|Chalmers distinguished the "easy problems" of consciousness, explaining abilities such as attention, memory and reporting, from the "hard problem": why there is felt experience, the redness of red or the hurt of pain. Some think science will eventually dissolve the problem; others that consciousness is a fundamental feature of reality. It is one of the great open questions about ourselves.
ideaphil|Gettier's Problem|You can have a true belief, with good reasons, and still not really know. A stopped clock that happens to show the right time doesn't give you knowledge.|Edmund Gettier||e|1963|For over two thousand years, many philosophers had defined knowledge as justified true belief. In a three-page paper Gettier described cases where all three conditions hold, yet the person is right only by luck. (The stopped-clock example is Bertrand Russell's.) Philosophers have searched ever since for the missing ingredient, and the paper is one of the most influential ever written in the field.
ideaphil|The Sorites Paradox|One grain of sand isn't a heap. Adding a single grain never turns a non-heap into a heap. So, it seems, there are no heaps at all.|Eubulides of Miletus||e|4th century BC|The paradox of the heap shows how vague words resist precise boundaries. The same reasoning works for "tall", "bald", "old" or "rich". There is no exact moment when day becomes night, yet the difference is real. Logicians still debate solutions, and the puzzle matters in law and medicine, where lines must be drawn on gradual scales.
ideaphil|The Liar Paradox|"This sentence is false." If it's true, it's false; if it's false, it's true. A simple sentence breaks logic.|Eubulides of Miletus||e|4th century BC|The liar paradox has puzzled thinkers from ancient Greece to medieval logicians and modern mathematicians. Its self-reference inspired Russell's paradox, Gödel's incompleteness theorems and Turing's proof that some problems are uncomputable. It shows that when language or logic talks about itself, strange and profound results can follow.
ideaphil|Natural Rights|Every person has rights to life, liberty and property by nature, not as gifts from a ruler. Governments exist to protect them, and lose their claim when they don't.|John Locke||e|1689|In his "Second Treatise of Government", Locke argued that political power comes from the consent of the governed, who form governments to secure rights they already have. A government that violates those rights may be resisted. His ideas shaped the American Declaration of Independence, the French Declaration of the Rights of Man and the modern idea of human rights.
ideaphil|The Rights of Woman|If women seem weaker in mind, it is because they are denied education. Give them reason and independence, and they will be companions, not ornaments.|Mary Wollstonecraft||e|1792|Wollstonecraft wrote "A Vindication of the Rights of Woman" in six weeks, answering those who said girls needed training only in charm and obedience. She argued that virtue requires reason, so denying women education denies them full humanity and harms society. Scorned by many at the time, she is now seen as a founder of feminist philosophy. Her daughter was Mary Shelley, author of "Frankenstein".
ideaphil|Care Ethics|Morality begins not with abstract rules but with relationships, with noticing and responding to the needs of those who depend on us.|Carol Gilligan and Nel Noddings||e|1982|Carol Gilligan noticed that theories of moral development, built mostly from studies of men, ranked justice and rules above care and connection. She described a "different voice" that sees morality in terms of responsibility and relationships. Nel Noddings and others developed this into an ethics of care, now applied to medicine, education and the work of looking after children, the sick and the old.
ideaphil|Attention Is Generosity|Truly attending to another person, waiting, emptying yourself of your own concerns, receiving them as they are, is the rarest and purest form of generosity.|Simone Weil||e|1940s|The French philosopher Simone Weil worked in factories and fields to understand suffering, and died at 34 in 1943. She saw attention as the heart of both prayer and love: the ability to look at someone in pain and ask, "What are you going through?" Her idea is that real help begins not with doing but with seeing.
ideamind|The Tip of the Tongue|We sometimes know a word is there, we can tell its first letter or how many syllables it has, and yet it will not come. Memory is stored in pieces, not whole.|Roger Brown and David McNeill||e|1966|Psychologists Roger Brown and David McNeill read definitions of rare words to students and studied what they could recall when the word was on the tip of the tongue. People often knew its sound, length and first letter. The experience happens to everyone, more often with age and tiredness, and usually resolves itself when we stop trying.
ideamind|Thinking Out Loud|Lev Vygotsky saw children talking to themselves as they play and work, and argued that this private speech gradually turns inward and becomes thought. We learn to think by first talking to others, then to ourselves.|Lev Vygotsky||e|1934|Lev Vygotsky, a Soviet psychologist who died young, wrote Thought and Language in 1934. He also introduced the zone of proximal development: what a learner can do with help today, they can do alone tomorrow. His ideas shaped modern education, emphasising guidance, conversation and collaboration in learning. Adults' inner voice may be a descendant of childhood talk.
ideamind|The Forgetting Curve|We forget most new information within days, fast at first and then more slowly. Reviewing it at growing intervals makes it stick.|Hermann Ebbinghaus||e|1885|Ebbinghaus memorised thousands of nonsense syllables and tested himself after different delays, creating the first experimental study of memory. He found that forgetting follows a steep, predictable curve, and that spaced repetition flattens it. Today's flashcard apps and language courses are built on his discovery. To learn something by heart, revisit it just as you are beginning to forget it.
ideamind|Remembering Is Rebuilding|Memory isn't a recording but a reconstruction. Each time we recall something, we rebuild it, and may change it without noticing.|Frederic Bartlett and Elizabeth Loftus||e|1932 / 1974|Bartlett asked English students to retell a Native American folk tale and found they reshaped it to fit their own culture. Decades later Elizabeth Loftus showed that a single word in a question, "smashed" instead of "hit", could change witnesses' memories of a car crash, and that false memories can be implanted. This keeps us humble about certainty, and reminds us that how we retell our past shapes it.
ideamind|The Magical Number Seven|We can hold only a handful of things in mind at once. Grouping them into meaningful chunks lets us hold much more.|George A. Miller||e|1956|In a playful paper, Miller noted that people can keep about seven items, give or take two, in short-term memory. Later research suggests the true number is closer to four. But we can stretch the limit by "chunking": a phone number becomes three groups, and a chess master sees a familiar position as a single pattern. Expertise is largely the building of bigger chunks.
ideamind|Deliberate Practice|Expertise comes less from talent or hours alone than from focused practice at the edge of your ability, with feedback, and repeated.|K. Anders Ericsson||e|1993|Ericsson studied violinists in Berlin and found that the best had spent the most time in solitary, effortful practice designed to improve weaknesses. His work was popularised as the "10,000-hour rule", which he said was an oversimplification. The core lesson stands: we improve most when we work on what we can't yet do, notice our mistakes and adjust.
ideamind|The Zone of Proximal Development|We learn best just beyond what we can do alone, with the help of someone a little further along.|Lev Vygotsky||e|1930s|The Soviet psychologist Vygotsky, who died at 37, saw learning as social. A child who can't solve a puzzle alone may manage it with a hint, and that zone between independent and assisted ability is where growth happens. Teachers and parents can offer "scaffolding" that is gradually removed. The idea applies to adults too: find people slightly ahead of you, and learn alongside them.
ideamind|Stages of Thinking|Children are not small adults. Their minds build understanding through stages, each with its own logic, from touching and grasping to abstract thought.|Jean Piaget||e|1920s–1950s|Piaget watched his own children and noticed that their errors followed patterns. A young child may think a tall, thin glass holds more water than a short, wide one with the same amount. He proposed that children actively construct knowledge by exploring. Later research found children more capable than he thought, but his view of the child as a little scientist transformed education.
ideamind|Life's Eight Stages|Each period of life brings its own crisis to work through, from trust in infancy to identity in youth, intimacy in adulthood and integrity in old age.|Erik Erikson||e|1950|Erikson, who coined the phrase "identity crisis", described eight stages across the whole lifespan. In middle age the task is generativity, caring for the next generation; in old age, finding integrity rather than despair in the life one has lived. His work helped psychologists see development as lifelong, not something that ends in childhood.
ideamind|Inattentional Blindness|When focused on one thing, we can miss something obvious right in front of us, even a person in a gorilla suit.|Daniel Simons and Christopher Chabris||e|1999|In a famous experiment, viewers counted basketball passes in a video. About half failed to notice a person in a gorilla costume walk through the scene and thump their chest. Attention is a spotlight, not a floodlight: we see what we look for. The study is a lesson in humility about how much we may be missing, and a reason to look again.
ideamind|Change Blindness|Swap a person in mid-conversation for someone else and most people won't notice. We take in far less detail than we think.|Daniel Simons and Daniel Levin||e|1998|In one study, an experimenter asked a passer-by for directions; when workers carrying a door passed between them, a different experimenter took his place. Half the participants didn't notice. Our sense of a rich, detailed visual world is partly an illusion, filled in by the brain's expectations. It explains why we miss gradual changes in loved ones, in ourselves and in society.
ideamind|Affective Forecasting|We are poor at predicting how future events will make us feel. Joys fade faster than we expect, and so do sorrows.|Daniel Gilbert and Timothy Wilson||e|1990s|Gilbert and Wilson found that people overestimate how long and how strongly they will be affected by good and bad events, from winning a prize to a break-up. We focus on the event and forget all the other things that will fill our days, and we underrate our "psychological immune system", our ability to make sense of setbacks. The finding is comforting: we are more resilient than we predict.
ideamind|The Extended Mind|Our minds don't stop at the skull. A notebook, a phone or a sketch can become part of how we think.|Andy Clark and David Chalmers||e|1998|Clark and Chalmers imagined Otto, who has memory loss and relies on a notebook, and Inga, who relies on biological memory. If both use their resource in the same way, why not say the notebook is part of Otto's mind? We think with our hands, tools, maps and conversations. The idea suggests choosing our tools and companions carefully, since they become part of how we think.
ideamind|Embodied Cognition|Thinking isn't only in the head. Posture, gesture, movement and the senses shape how we reason, feel and remember.|George Lakoff, Mark Johnson, Francisco Varela and others||e|1980s–1990s|Researchers found that gesturing helps children learn maths, that walking can boost creative thinking, and that we understand abstract ideas through bodily metaphors, like feeling "close" to someone or "grasping" an idea. Varela, Thompson and Rosch's "The Embodied Mind" linked these findings to Buddhist ideas. Mind and body, it turns out, are not separate partners but one system.
ideamind|Emotions Are Constructed|The brain doesn't simply detect emotions; it builds them, combining body sensations with past experience and the concepts we have learned.|Lisa Feldman Barrett||e|2017|Barrett's research challenged the idea that each emotion has a fixed fingerprint in the face and brain. Instead, the brain predicts and makes sense of bodily feelings using emotion concepts. People who can name their feelings more precisely, distinguishing "disappointed" from "lonely", tend to cope better. Learning new words for feelings may literally expand what we can feel and manage.
ideamind|Broaden and Build|Positive emotions widen our attention and openness, and over time they build lasting resources: skills, friendships and resilience.|Barbara Fredrickson||e|1998|Fear narrows our focus to fight or flight. Fredrickson proposed that emotions like joy, interest and contentment do the opposite: they broaden what we notice and want to try. Playing, exploring and connecting, done again and again, build a store of abilities and relationships we can draw on in hard times. Good feelings, in this view, are not trivial but a kind of growth.
ideamind|The Incubation Effect|Stuck on a problem? Step away. The mind keeps working underneath, and solutions often appear in the shower or on a walk.|Graham Wallas and Henri Poincaré||e|1908 / 1926|The mathematician Poincaré described how the solution to a problem he had struggled with came to him as he stepped onto a bus, while thinking of something else. Graham Wallas named four stages of creativity: preparation, incubation, illumination and verification. Experiments suggest that breaks, especially with undemanding activity, do help with creative problems. Rest is part of thinking.
ideamind|Meaning Makes Suffering Bearable|We can endure almost any hardship if we find meaning in it. Even when everything else is taken, we keep the freedom to choose our attitude.|Viktor Frankl||e|1946|Frankl, a Viennese psychiatrist, survived Auschwitz and other Nazi camps, which took the lives of his wife, parents and brother. In "Man's Search for Meaning" he described how prisoners who held onto a purpose, a loved one, a task or a faith, were more likely to endure. He founded logotherapy, which helps people find meaning through work, love and the courage to face unavoidable suffering.
ideasci|The Quantum|Energy is not smooth like water but comes in tiny packets. Max Planck called them quanta, and the whole strange world of quantum physics grew from that one reluctant guess.|Max Planck||e|1900|In 1900 Max Planck was trying to explain the colours of light given off by hot objects. The only formula that matched the experiments assumed energy is released in small fixed amounts, each proportional to the frequency of the light. Planck thought of it as a mathematical trick, but it turned out to describe nature itself. The tiny number in his formula, Planck's constant, sets the scale where the quantum world begins.
ideasci|Light Comes in Particles|Light, long known to be a wave, also arrives in separate particles, photons. Shine light on a metal and the energy of each photon, not the brightness, decides whether electrons fly out.|Albert Einstein||e|1905|In his "miracle year" of 1905, Einstein explained the photoelectric effect: dim blue light can knock electrons out of a metal while bright red light cannot. This only makes sense if light comes in packets whose energy depends on colour. It was this work, not relativity, that won him the Nobel Prize, and it is how solar panels and camera sensors work today.
ideasci|Quantum Leaps|Electrons in an atom can only sit in certain orbits, like steps on a staircase. They jump between them without passing through the space in between, giving out or taking in light of an exact colour.|Niels Bohr||e|1913|Niels Bohr proposed that an atom's electrons have only certain allowed energies. When one drops to a lower level it gives off a photon of one precise colour, which is why every element has its own barcode of spectral lines. The picture of tiny orbits was later replaced by clouds of probability, but the idea of fixed levels and sudden jumps remains at the heart of chemistry.
ideasci|The Double-Slit Experiment|Fire single particles, one at a time, at a wall with two slits, and they build up a pattern of stripes, as if each one went through both slits and interfered with itself. Watch which slit they use and the stripes vanish.|Thomas Young; quantum physicists since||e|1801 / 1920s|Thomas Young first used two slits in 1801 to show that light is a wave. In the twentieth century the experiment was done with electrons, then single electrons, atoms and even large molecules, with the same result. Richard Feynman said it holds "the only mystery" of quantum mechanics: nothing we picture from everyday life explains how a single particle can behave like a spreading wave until it is observed.
ideasci|Superposition|Until it is measured, a quantum system can be in a blend of possibilities at once: here and there, spinning up and down. Measurement gives one definite answer, with odds the theory predicts exactly.|Erwin Schrödinger, Paul Dirac and others||e|1920s|In quantum mechanics a particle is described by a wave function that can combine several states. This is not ignorance about which state it is "really" in: the possibilities can interfere with each other, which is what the double-slit stripes show. What turns the blend into one outcome when we look is the measurement problem, still debated a century later.
ideasci|Schrödinger's Cat|A cat is sealed in a box with a device that may or may not be triggered by one radioactive atom. If quantum rules applied to everything, the cat would be both alive and dead until someone opens the box.|Erwin Schrödinger||e|1935|Schrödinger meant the cat as a joke at quantum theory's expense: an absurd result to show that something was missing in how we understand measurement. It became the most famous image of the quantum world instead. Physicists still argue over where the line between the quantum and the everyday lies, and experiments now put ever larger objects into superposition.
ideasci|Complementarity|Light is a wave and a particle, but no single experiment can show both faces at once. The full truth needs two pictures that seem to contradict each other.|Niels Bohr||e|1927|Bohr argued that wave and particle are complementary descriptions: each is true in its own setting, and together they are complete. He saw the idea as wider than physics, and chose for his coat of arms the yin-yang symbol with the motto "Contraria sunt complementa", opposites are complementary. It is a lesson in holding two truths at once.
ideasci|Quantum Tunnelling|A particle can pass through a barrier it doesn't have the energy to climb, like a ball rolling through a hill. The Sun only shines because protons tunnel close enough to fuse.|George Gamow and others||e|1928|Because a particle is spread out like a wave, a little of it always leaks through a barrier, so there is a small chance it turns up on the other side. George Gamow used this to explain radioactive decay. In the Sun's core, protons don't have enough energy to overcome their repulsion, yet some tunnel through and fuse, releasing the light that reaches us. Tunnelling also makes flash memory and scanning tunnelling microscopes work.
ideasci|Why Matter Is Solid|No two electrons can be in exactly the same state. This one rule, the exclusion principle, stops atoms collapsing into each other and gives every element its character.|Wolfgang Pauli||e|1925|Pauli's exclusion principle means electrons have to stack up in shells around the nucleus instead of all crowding into the lowest level. That arrangement gives the periodic table its pattern and chemistry its variety. It is also why your hand doesn't sink through a table, and what holds up white dwarfs and neutron stars against their own gravity.
ideasci|Antimatter|For every kind of particle there is a mirror twin with the opposite charge. When matter meets antimatter, both vanish in a flash of pure energy.|Paul Dirac||e|1928|Paul Dirac wrote an equation joining quantum mechanics and special relativity, and found it had two kinds of solution. Instead of discarding the strange ones, he predicted a positive electron. Carl Anderson found it in cosmic rays in 1932. Hospitals now use antimatter in PET scans. Why the universe is made almost entirely of matter, when the Big Bang should have made equal amounts of both, is still one of physics' great open questions.
ideasci|The Restless Vacuum|Empty space is never truly empty. Quantum fields flicker even in a perfect vacuum, and two metal plates placed close together are gently pushed towards each other by its pressure.|Hendrik Casimir||e|1948|The uncertainty principle means fields can never be perfectly still, so even empty space has a restless "zero-point" energy. Hendrik Casimir predicted that two uncharged plates very close together would be pushed together because fewer fluctuations fit between them than outside. The force has since been measured. Nothingness, in modern physics, is full of activity.
ideasci|Bell's Theorem|John Bell found a test that could tell whether quantum correlations come from hidden instructions carried by particles. Experiments say no: nature really is as strange as quantum theory says.|John Bell; Alain Aspect, John Clauser and Anton Zeilinger||e|1964 / 1982|Einstein hoped quantum randomness hid a deeper, ordinary reality. John Bell showed this could be tested: if particles carried hidden local instructions, certain correlations could never exceed a limit. Experiments from the 1970s onward broke that limit, again and again, with the loopholes closed one by one. Aspect, Clauser and Zeilinger shared the 2022 Nobel Prize for this work, which also laid the ground for quantum cryptography.
ideasci|Decoherence|Why don't we see cats that are both alive and dead? Because the world keeps peeking. Every stray photon and air molecule that touches a large object quickly washes out its quantum blend.|H. Dieter Zeh and Wojciech Zurek||e|1970s–80s|Decoherence explains why quantum strangeness is hidden at everyday scales. A big object is constantly interacting with its surroundings, which records its state and makes the different possibilities stop interfering with each other almost instantly. It doesn't fully solve the measurement problem, but it explains why the classical world looks solid and definite, and why building quantum computers, which must be shielded from the world, is so hard.
ideasci|Everything Is Fields|The universe isn't made of tiny balls. It is made of fields filling all of space, and what we call particles are ripples in them: an electron is a ripple in the electron field, light a ripple in the electromagnetic field.|Paul Dirac, Richard Feynman, Julian Schwinger, Sin-Itiro Tomonaga and others||e|1927–1950s|Quantum field theory joins quantum mechanics with special relativity. Every electron in the universe is identical because each is a ripple in the same field. Its first complete version, quantum electrodynamics, predicts the magnetism of the electron to better than one part in a billion, making it the most precisely tested theory in all of science.
ideasci|Every Possible Path|To get from here to there, a particle in a sense takes every possible path at once. The paths add up, most cancel out, and what is left looks like the single route we see.|Richard Feynman||e|1948|Feynman found a new way to do quantum mechanics: add up a contribution from every conceivable path a particle could take, each with its own phase. Wild paths cancel each other, while paths near the classical one reinforce, which is why a thrown ball seems to follow one neat curve. His little diagrams for these calculations became the everyday language of particle physics.
ideasci|The Higgs Field|An invisible field fills the universe, and particles get their mass by interacting with it. Without it, electrons would fly at the speed of light and atoms could never form.|Peter Higgs, François Englert and others||e|1964 / 2012|In 1964 several physicists proposed that the universe is filled with a field that gives the fundamental particles their mass. Its ripple, the Higgs boson, was found at CERN's Large Hadron Collider in 2012, nearly fifty years later. Most of your own mass, though, comes not from the Higgs but from the energy of quarks and gluons bound inside protons and neutrons, a living example of E = mc².
ideasci|Ghost Particles|About a hundred trillion neutrinos from the Sun pass through your body every second, day and night, and almost none of them touch you. They pass through the whole Earth as if it weren't there.|Wolfgang Pauli, Enrico Fermi, Clyde Cowan and Frederick Reines||e|1930 / 1956|Pauli proposed the neutrino in 1930 to balance the energy in radioactive decay, apologising for inventing a particle that could never be detected. Cowan and Reines detected it in 1956 beside a nuclear reactor. Neutrinos are so light and aloof that detectors need thousands of tonnes of water deep underground. In 1987 neutrinos from a supernova arrived hours before its light.
ideasci|The Standard Model|Seventeen particles and three forces account for every experiment ever done on Earth. It is the most successful theory in history, and physicists know it is incomplete.|Sheldon Glashow, Abdus Salam, Steven Weinberg and many others||e|1970s|The Standard Model describes quarks, electrons, neutrinos and the particles that carry the electromagnetic, weak and strong forces, completed by the Higgs boson. It has passed every test at particle colliders. Yet it leaves out gravity, says nothing about dark matter or dark energy, and doesn't explain why there is more matter than antimatter. The search for what lies beyond it continues.
ideasci|Quantum Computing|A quantum computer uses superposition and entanglement to explore many possibilities in a single calculation, solving some problems that would take ordinary computers longer than the age of the universe.|Richard Feynman, David Deutsch, Peter Shor and others||e|1981 onward|Feynman suggested in 1981 that nature, being quantum, is best simulated by a quantum machine. Its qubits can hold blends of 0 and 1, and clever algorithms make the wrong answers cancel and the right ones reinforce. Peter Shor showed in 1994 that such a machine could break much of today's encryption. Real machines exist, but keeping qubits shielded from decoherence remains the great engineering challenge.
ideasci|The Smallest Length|There may be a smallest meaningful distance, the Planck length, about a hundred billion billion times smaller than a proton. Below it, space and time themselves may stop making sense.|Max Planck; quantum gravity research||e|1899 onward|Combining the speed of light, gravity's strength and Planck's constant gives a length of about 10⁻³⁵ metres. At that scale the quantum jitter of spacetime should become as large as the distance being measured, and our theories break down. Joining general relativity and quantum mechanics into one theory of quantum gravity, through string theory, loop quantum gravity or something not yet imagined, is the deepest unsolved problem in physics.
ideasci|Reading Starlight|Spread a star's light into a rainbow and dark lines appear, the fingerprints of the elements it contains. We know what stars are made of without ever touching one.|Joseph von Fraunhofer, Gustav Kirchhoff and Robert Bunsen||e|1814 / 1859|In 1835 the philosopher Auguste Comte gave the chemical makeup of stars as an example of something we could never know. Within a generation, Kirchhoff and Bunsen showed that each element absorbs its own precise colours, and astronomers read the Sun's ingredients from the dark lines Fraunhofer had mapped. Helium was discovered in the Sun before it was found on Earth. Spectra also tell us a star's temperature, speed and motion.
ideasci|The Sun Is Mostly Hydrogen|The stars are not made of the same stuff as the Earth. They are almost entirely hydrogen and helium, the simplest elements of all.|Cecilia Payne-Gaposchkin||e|1925|In her 1925 doctoral thesis, Cecilia Payne used quantum physics to read stellar spectra properly and concluded that stars are mostly hydrogen. Senior astronomers thought she must be wrong, and she was persuaded to call her result "almost certainly not real". Four years later it was accepted. Her thesis has been called the most brilliant ever written in astronomy.
ideasci|The Death of Stars|A star like the Sun ends gently, shedding its outer layers into a glowing nebula and leaving a white dwarf. A heavier star ends in a supernova, an explosion that can outshine a whole galaxy.|Subrahmanyan Chandrasekhar and others||e|1930 onward|Stars live by balancing the inward pull of gravity against the outward push of the heat of fusion. When the fuel runs out, the balance fails. On a voyage to England at nineteen, Chandrasekhar calculated that above about 1.4 times the Sun's mass, a dying core can't be held up as a white dwarf and must collapse further, into a neutron star or a black hole. The elements scattered by those explosions seed new stars, planets and, in time, us.
ideasci|Light Bends Around the Sun|Gravity bends light. During a total eclipse in 1919, stars near the Sun appeared shifted from their usual places, just as Einstein had predicted.|Albert Einstein and Arthur Eddington||e|1919|General relativity predicted that light passing the Sun would bend by twice the amount Newton's theory suggested. Expeditions led by Arthur Eddington photographed the eclipse from Príncipe and Brazil, and the result made Einstein world-famous overnight. Today whole galaxies act as gravitational lenses, magnifying and distorting more distant ones, and lensing is one of our best ways to map invisible dark matter.
ideasci|Time Runs at Different Speeds|Time passes more slowly for a clock that moves fast or sits deep in gravity. An astronaut returning from a long, fast trip would be younger than their twin who stayed home.|Albert Einstein||e|1905 / 1915|Special relativity shows that moving clocks run slow, and general relativity that clocks lower in a gravitational field run slow too. These are not tricks of measurement: time itself flows differently. GPS satellites must correct for both effects, by about 38 microseconds a day, or your phone's map would drift by kilometres. There is no single "now" shared by the whole universe.
ideasci|A Picture of a Black Hole|In 2019 we saw, for the first time, the shadow of a black hole: a dark centre ringed by glowing gas, in a galaxy 55 million light years away.|The Event Horizon Telescope collaboration||e|2019|No single telescope could see something so small and far away, so radio dishes across the Earth were linked to act as one telescope the size of the planet. The image of the black hole in the galaxy M87 matched Einstein's predictions closely. In 2022 the same team released an image of Sagittarius A*, the black hole at the centre of our own galaxy.
ideasci|The First Three Minutes|In the first few minutes after the Big Bang, the universe was a nuclear furnace. It made almost all the hydrogen and helium there is, in a ratio that astronomers still measure today.|George Gamow, Ralph Alpher and Robert Herman; Steven Weinberg||e|1948 / 1977|As the young universe cooled, protons and neutrons fused into helium nuclei, until expansion made it too cold and thin to continue. The theory predicts about three parts hydrogen to one part helium by mass, with traces of lithium, and that is what we observe in the oldest stars and gas clouds. Every hydrogen atom in the water you drink was made in those first minutes, 13.8 billion years ago.
ideasci|Cosmic Dawn|For hundreds of millions of years after the Big Bang there were no stars, only dark, cooling gas. Then the first stars ignited and the universe lit up.|Modern cosmology; the James Webb Space Telescope||e|c. 100–400 million years after the Big Bang|After the afterglow of the Big Bang faded, the universe went through its "dark ages". Gravity slowly pulled gas into clumps until the first stars switched on, huge and short-lived, made only of hydrogen and helium. Their light and explosions reshaped the cosmos. The James Webb Space Telescope, launched in 2021, is now seeing galaxies from this era, earlier and brighter than many expected.
ideasci|Our Cosmic Address|The Milky Way is one of about a hundred thousand galaxies flowing together in a vast supercluster named Laniakea, Hawaiian for "immeasurable heaven".|R. Brent Tully and colleagues||e|2014|By mapping the motions of thousands of galaxies, astronomers drew the boundaries of the supercluster we belong to: a basin of gravity about 500 million light years across. Our full address is now something like: Earth, the Solar System, the Orion Arm, the Milky Way, the Local Group, the Virgo Supercluster, Laniakea, the observable universe.
ideasci|Quasars|Some of the brightest objects in the universe are the hearts of distant galaxies, where gas swirling into a giant black hole glows brighter than a trillion suns.|Maarten Schmidt and others||e|1963|In 1963 Maarten Schmidt realised that a faint, star-like point of light, 3C 273, was actually about two billion light years away, which meant it had to be impossibly bright. Quasars turned out to be supermassive black holes feeding on matter, which heats up as it spirals in. Paradoxically, the darkest objects in nature power the brightest.
ideasci|Wormholes|General relativity allows tunnels through spacetime that could link distant places, or even times. No one knows whether nature makes them.|Albert Einstein and Nathan Rosen; Kip Thorne||e|1935 / 1988|Einstein and Rosen found that the equations allow a "bridge" between two regions of space. Later work showed such bridges would pinch off too quickly for anything to pass, unless held open by exotic matter with negative energy. Kip Thorne studied what it would take, partly to help Carl Sagan with the novel Contact. Physicists now suspect a deep link between wormholes and quantum entanglement.
ideasci|The Holographic Principle|Everything inside a region of space may be fully described by information on its boundary, the way a flat hologram holds a 3-D image. Our three-dimensional world may, in a sense, be a projection.|Gerard 't Hooft, Leonard Susskind and Juan Maldacena||e|1993 / 1997|The idea grew from the discovery that the information a black hole can hold depends on the area of its surface, not its volume. 't Hooft and Susskind proposed this is true of any region of space. In 1997 Maldacena found a precise example, in which a universe with gravity is exactly equivalent to a quantum theory without gravity on its edge. It is one of the most fruitful clues to how space itself might emerge.
ideasci|The Multiverse|If cosmic inflation never fully stops, it may endlessly bud off new universes, each a bubble with its own history and perhaps its own laws of physics. Ours would be one among countless others.|Andrei Linde, Alexander Vilenkin and others||e|1980s|In the theory of eternal inflation, the rapid expansion that smoothed our early universe carries on forever somewhere, and bubbles like ours form within it. Combined with string theory, this suggests the laws we see may be local customs, not universal rules. Critics point out that other universes may be impossible to observe, which makes it a question at the edge of what science can test.
ideasci|Mutual Aid|"Don't compete!—competition is always injurious to the species, and you have plenty of resources to avoid it!" That is the tendency of nature, not always realized in full, but always present.|Peter Kropotkin, Mutual Aid: A Factor of Evolution (1902)|Kropotkin, a naturalist as well as a revolutionary, saw in Siberia that animals survive hard conditions by cooperating. Evolution rewards helping as well as competing.|q|1902|Peter Kropotkin was a Russian prince turned geographer and anarchist. Travelling in Siberia, he expected to see animals locked in a brutal fight for survival but instead saw herds, flocks and colonies surviving the harsh climate by helping one another. In "Mutual Aid" he argued that cooperation is a factor of evolution as important as competition, in animals and in human history alike. Modern biology, with its studies of kin selection, reciprocity and symbiosis, has largely borne him out.
ideasci|Atoms|Everything is made of tiny particles in endless motion, combining and separating. What began as a philosopher's guess became the foundation of chemistry.|Democritus and John Dalton||e|5th century BC / 1808|Leucippus and Democritus proposed that matter is made of indivisible atoms moving through empty space. For two thousand years it was speculation. In 1808 John Dalton showed that elements combine in fixed ratios, explained by atoms of different weights. Einstein's 1905 analysis of Brownian motion, the jiggling of pollen grains in water, finally convinced the last sceptics that atoms are real.
ideasci|The Laws of Motion|An object keeps moving in a straight line unless a force acts on it; force equals mass times acceleration; every action has an equal and opposite reaction.|Isaac Newton||e|1687|Newton's three laws, set out in the "Principia", explained everything from a falling apple to the paths of comets with the same few principles. The first overturned the ancient belief that moving things naturally slow down. Together with his law of gravity, they let people predict the heavens and build the machines of the industrial age, and they still launch every spacecraft.
ideasci|The Square-Cube Law|Double an animal's size and its strength grows four times but its weight eight times. That is why there are no giant ants, and why elephants have thick legs.|Galileo Galilei||e|1638|In "Two New Sciences" Galileo showed that size alone changes what is possible. Strength depends on the cross-section of bones and muscles, which grows with the square of size, while weight grows with the cube. A giant built to human proportions would collapse under itself. The same law explains why small animals lose heat fast, why insects can fall safely and why big ships and buildings need different designs.
ideasci|Lever and Fulcrum|"Give me a place to stand, and I will move the Earth." With a long enough lever, a small force can lift an enormous weight.|Archimedes||e|3rd century BC|Archimedes worked out the mathematics of the lever: weight times distance on one side balances weight times distance on the other. The boast about moving the Earth is recorded by later writers. He also discovered how floating works and is said to have run through Syracuse crying "Eureka!" The principle of leverage now describes everything from scissors to finance and personal effort.
ideasci|Electromagnetic Induction|Move a magnet near a coil of wire and electricity flows. This simple discovery powers almost every generator on Earth.|Michael Faraday||e|1831|Faraday, the son of a blacksmith with little schooling, became one of the greatest experimenters in history. He showed that a changing magnetic field creates an electric current, imagining invisible "lines of force" filling space. His work led to electric motors, generators and transformers, and inspired Maxwell's theory of light.
ideasci|The Greenhouse Effect|Gases such as carbon dioxide and water vapour let sunlight in but trap some of the heat trying to escape, warming the Earth like a blanket.|Eunice Foote, John Tyndall and Svante Arrhenius||e|1856 / 1859 / 1896|In 1856 the American scientist Eunice Foote showed that a jar of carbon dioxide heats up more in sunlight than ordinary air. John Tyndall measured how gases absorb heat radiation. In 1896 Svante Arrhenius calculated that doubling carbon dioxide would warm the planet by several degrees. Without the natural greenhouse effect, Earth would be frozen; adding to it, as burning fossil fuels does, is warming the world.
ideasci|Ice Ages and the Wobbling Earth|Slow cycles in Earth's orbit and tilt, lasting tens of thousands of years, change how sunlight falls on the planet, and drive the coming and going of ice ages.|Milutin Milanković||e|1920s–1941|The Serbian engineer Milankovic spent decades calculating by hand how changes in the shape of Earth's orbit, its tilt and its wobble alter summer sunshine in the north. He proposed that these cycles pace the ice ages. In the 1970s, layers in deep-sea sediments confirmed his rhythms of about 100,000, 41,000 and 23,000 years. The heavens, it turns out, set the climate's long tempo.
ideasci|Sexual Selection|Some features evolve not to help survival but to win mates: the peacock's tail, the songbird's song, the stag's antlers.|Charles Darwin||e|1871|The peacock's tail troubled Darwin, since it seemed only to make the bird easier for predators to catch. In "The Descent of Man" he argued that competition for mates, and the choices of females, drive the evolution of ornaments and displays. The idea was neglected for a century but is now central to biology, and helps explain the diversity and beauty of the living world.
ideasci|The Red Queen|Species must keep evolving just to stay in place, because their predators, prey and parasites are evolving too.|Leigh Van Valen||e|1973|In Lewis Carroll's "Through the Looking-Glass", the Red Queen tells Alice, "It takes all the running you can do, to keep in the same place." The biologist Leigh Van Valen borrowed the image to explain why extinction risk doesn't decline with a group's age: the race never ends. It may even explain why sex exists, since mixing genes helps offspring keep ahead of fast-evolving parasites.
ideasci|Convergent Evolution|Unrelated creatures facing the same problem often evolve the same solution. Dolphins and sharks share a shape; octopuses and humans have camera eyes.|Evolutionary biology||e|19th century onward|Wings evolved separately in insects, birds, bats and pterosaurs. Echolocation arose in bats and whales. The physics of water, air and light rewards certain designs, and natural selection keeps finding them. Some scientists argue that convergence shows evolution is less random than it looks, and that life elsewhere might resemble life here in surprising ways.
ideasci|Kin Selection|Helping relatives can spread your genes, because they share many of them. It helps explain why bees die for their hive and parents sacrifice for children.|W. D. Hamilton||e|1964|The puzzle of altruism troubled Darwin: how could self-sacrifice evolve? Hamilton showed mathematically that a gene for helping can spread if the benefit to relatives, weighted by how closely related they are, outweighs the cost. J. B. S. Haldane joked that he would lay down his life for two brothers or eight cousins. Kin selection is one of several routes, with reciprocity and group living, by which cooperation evolves.
ideasci|The Origins of Life|Lightning in a flask of simple gases produced amino acids, the building blocks of life. The chemistry of life may arise naturally from ordinary matter.|Stanley Miller and Harold Urey||e|1952|Miller, a graduate student, passed sparks through methane, ammonia, hydrogen and water for a week and found the brown residue rich in amino acids. Scientists now think early Earth's atmosphere was different, and many look to deep-sea vents or warm ponds, but the experiment showed that life's ingredients form easily. How chemistry first became biology remains one of science's greatest open questions.
ideasci|The Genetic Code|Every living thing uses the same code: three-letter words in DNA, each specifying one amino acid. From bacteria to blue whales, life speaks one language.|Marshall Nirenberg, Har Gobind Khorana and others||e|1961–1966|After the double helix, scientists raced to learn how DNA's four letters spell out proteins. Nirenberg and Heinrich Matthaei cracked the first word in 1961; by 1966 all 64 had been deciphered. That the code is nearly universal is powerful evidence that all life shares a common ancestor. It also lets genes from one creature work in another, the basis of biotechnology.
ideasci|Gene Editing|Bacteria fight viruses by cutting their DNA at precise points. Scientists turned that defence into a tool to rewrite genes.|Jennifer Doudna and Emmanuelle Charpentier||e|2012|CRISPR began as a strange repeating pattern in bacterial DNA, which turned out to be a memory of past viral attacks. Doudna and Charpentier showed that the system could be programmed to cut any DNA sequence, and they shared the 2020 Nobel Prize in Chemistry. It is already curing some genetic blood disorders, and it raises deep ethical questions about changing human heredity.
ideasci|Circadian Rhythms|Almost every cell in your body keeps time on a roughly 24-hour clock, set each day by light. Sleep, hunger, mood and alertness all follow its beat.|Jean-Jacques d'Ortous de Mairan and modern chronobiology||e|1729 / 2017|In 1729 the French astronomer de Mairan noticed that a mimosa plant kept opening and closing its leaves on schedule even in a dark cupboard. Centuries later, Jeffrey Hall, Michael Rosbash and Michael Young uncovered the genes behind the clock, earning the 2017 Nobel Prize. Morning light, regular sleep and meal times help keep the body's clocks in tune.
ideasci|Sleep Cleans and Consolidates|While we sleep, the brain replays the day's learning to strengthen memories and flushes out waste that builds up while we are awake.|Modern sleep science||e|1950s–2013|Sleep was once thought to be simply rest. Researchers discovered rapid eye movement sleep in 1953, then found that sleep strengthens memories and skills. In 2013 Maiken Nedergaard's team found that the brain's waste-clearing system works much harder during sleep. Sleep, it turns out, is an active state essential to learning, health and mood.
ideasci|The Scientific Method|Make a guess, work out what it predicts, test it against nature, and change your mind if the evidence says so. Knowledge grows by checking.|Ibn al-Haytham, Francis Bacon, Galileo and others||e|11th–17th century|There is no single inventor of the scientific method. Ibn al-Haytham, in his 11th-century "Book of Optics", insisted on testing claims by experiment and being critical even of authorities. Bacon championed careful observation; Galileo combined experiment with mathematics. The method's strength is not certainty but self-correction: science is the organised habit of finding out where we are wrong.
ideasci|Chance Favours the Prepared Mind|Discoveries seem to come by accident, but only to those who have done the work to recognise them.|Louis Pasteur||e|1854|Pasteur said this in a lecture, noting that in observation chance favours only the prepared mind. Fleming's mouldy dish that led to penicillin, the hum of radio static that revealed the Big Bang, and Röntgen's glowing screen that showed X-rays were all lucky, but only to people ready to notice. Serendipity is real, and it rewards curiosity, knowledge and attention.
mythworld|The Hound of Culann|As a boy, Sétanta killed the fierce guard dog of the smith Culann in self-defence. Seeing the smith's grief, he offered to guard his house himself until a new hound was raised, and so took the name Cú Chulainn, the Hound of Culann.|Irish myth, the Ulster Cycle||e|Ireland|Cú Chulainn is the great hero of the Ulster Cycle, the Achilles of Irish legend. The story of his name is told in the Táin Bó Cúailnge. What it remembers is not his strength but his sense of fairness: he had done harm, so he made it good with his own service. Later tales show him defending Ulster alone at a river ford, bound by honour and by the geasa, the sacred taboos laid on him.
mythworld|The Dagda's Harp|The Dagda, the good god of the Irish, had a harp that played three kinds of music: the music of sorrow, which made all who heard it weep; the music of joy, which made them laugh; and the music of sleep, which sent them into rest.|Irish myth||e|Ireland|When the Fomorians stole the Dagda's harp, he called it from their hall and it flew to him, killing nine of them on the way. Then he played the three strains, and while his enemies slept, he and his companions escaped. The Dagda also owned a cauldron from which no one left hungry. Weeping, laughter and sleep: the old story names the three things music does to us.
mythworld|The Voyage of Bran|Bran mac Febail was visited by a woman from the Otherworld who sang of an island of endless summer. He sailed west with his companions, and stayed there what seemed a year. When they came home, no one knew them, except as an old story.|Irish myth, Immram Brain||e|early medieval Ireland|The Voyage of Bran is one of the immrama, Irish tales of sea voyages to wondrous islands. When one sailor jumped ashore on his return, he crumbled into ashes, for hundreds of years had passed. Bran wrote down his tale in ogham and sailed away again, never to be seen. Such stories mingle pagan Otherworlds with Christian pilgrim voyages like that of Saint Brendan.
mythworld|Ceridwen's Cauldron|The Welsh enchantress Ceridwen brewed a potion of wisdom for her son, to be stirred for a year and a day. Three drops splashed onto the hand of the boy Gwion Bach, who sucked his thumb and knew everything. He fled, she pursued, and through many shapes he was reborn as the poet Taliesin.|Welsh myth, Hanes Taliesin||e|Wales|In the chase, Gwion became a hare, a fish, a bird and finally a grain of wheat; Ceridwen became a greyhound, an otter, a hawk and a hen, and swallowed the grain. Nine months later she gave birth to a boy so beautiful she could not kill him, and set him adrift in a bag. Found in a fishing weir, he was named Taliesin, "radiant brow", and became the greatest of the bards.
mythworld|The Selkie's Skin|In the islands of Scotland and Ireland, seals could shed their skins and dance as people on the shore. A man who hid a selkie's skin could keep her as his wife, but if she ever found it again, she would return to the sea.|Scottish and Irish folklore||e|Orkney, Shetland and the Irish coast|Selkie stories are told across the northern islands. Usually the selkie wife lives for years on land and raises children, yet always gazes at the sea. One day a child finds the hidden skin and shows it to her, and she goes back to the waves, sometimes returning to watch her children from the water. They are stories about love that cannot be owned, and about longing for where we belong.
mythworld|Savitri and Yama|When her husband Satyavan died, Princess Savitri followed Yama, the lord of death, and would not turn back. Her wisdom and devotion pleased him so much that he granted her wishes, until she won back her husband's life.|The Mahabharata||e|ancient India|The tale is told in the Mahabharata's Forest Book. Savitri had chosen Satyavan knowing he was fated to die within a year. As Yama carried his soul away, she walked behind him, speaking so wisely of goodness that he offered her any boon except Satyavan's life. Cleverly, she asked for sons by her husband, and Yama, bound by his word, had to return him. She is honoured in India as a model of courage and steadfast love.
mythworld|Krishna Lifts the Mountain|When the storm god Indra sent a week of rain to punish the cowherds of Vrindavan, the young Krishna lifted Govardhan hill on one finger and held it up like an umbrella, sheltering every person and animal beneath it.|Hindu myth, the Bhagavata Purana||e|ancient India|Krishna had told the villagers to honour the hill that fed their cows, rather than offering sacrifices to Indra. Indra's anger brought the downpour; Krishna's answer was to protect them. After seven days Indra gave up and bowed to him. The story is celebrated each autumn at the festival of Govardhan Puja, when worshippers build small hills of food, a reminder that the divine is found in caring for what is near.
mythworld|The Dance of Shiva|As Nataraja, the lord of dance, Shiva dances within a ring of fire. One hand holds the drum that beats out creation, another the flame of destruction, while his foot rests on the dwarf of ignorance and his raised hand says: do not fear.|Hindu myth and art||e|South India, from about the 10th century|The famous bronzes of Shiva Nataraja come from the Chola kingdom of South India. His dance, the tandava, is the rhythm of the universe itself: worlds are made, kept and dissolved, over and over. The raised leg promises release. A two-metre statue of Nataraja stands at CERN, the physics laboratory in Geneva, as a symbol of the cosmic dance of particles.
mythworld|The Descent of the Ganges|King Bhagiratha prayed for a thousand years for the heavenly river Ganga to come down to earth. Her fall would have shattered the world, so Shiva caught her in his tangled hair, and let her flow out gently in streams.|Hindu myth, the Ramayana and the Puranas||e|ancient India|Bhagiratha needed the river's waters to purify the ashes of his sixty thousand ancestors. Ganga, proud of her power, meant to sweep away the earth, but Shiva held her in his locks until she was humbled. To this day the Ganges is called Bhagirathi near its source, and great effort in a good cause is called a "Bhagiratha effort".
mythworld|Durga and the Buffalo Demon|The buffalo demon Mahishasura could not be defeated by any god or man. So the gods poured their powers together, and from their light arose Durga, riding a lion, with a weapon in each of her ten hands. After nine days of battle she struck him down.|Hindu myth, the Devi Mahatmya||e|about 5th–6th century|The Devi Mahatmya tells how Durga, the invincible, was born from the united anger of the gods. Mahishasura changed shape again and again, buffalo, lion, man, elephant, but she overcame him in every form. Her victory is celebrated at Navaratri and Durga Puja, one of the great festivals of India and Bengal, as the triumph of good over evil and of the divine feminine.
mythworld|Urashima Tarō|A young fisherman rescued a turtle, which carried him to the Dragon Palace under the sea. He stayed three happy years with the princess Otohime. When he came home, three hundred years had passed, and he opened the box she had told him never to open.|Japanese folktale||e|Japan, first written in the 8th century|Otohime had given Urashima a lacquered box, the tamatebako, as a parting gift. Finding his village gone and everyone he knew long dead, he opened it in his grief. A white smoke rose, and he became an old man. Versions of the tale appear in the Nihon Shoki and the Man'yōshū. Like the Irish voyage of Bran, it is a story about time, and how we cannot go back.
mythworld|Momotarō, the Peach Boy|An old childless couple found a giant peach floating down the river. Inside was a baby boy, Momotarō. When he grew up, he set off to defeat the ogres of Onigashima, sharing his millet dumplings with a dog, a monkey and a pheasant, who became his companions.|Japanese folktale||e|Japan, Edo period|Momotarō is one of the best loved Japanese tales. The animals, won over by kindness and a shared meal, help him storm the ogres' island, and he brings their treasure home to his parents. The town of Okayama, famous for its peaches, claims him as its own. The story is about courage, gratitude and the power of friends who are very different from each other.
mythworld|Susanoo and the Eight-Headed Serpent|Banished from heaven, the storm god Susanoo met an old couple weeping: the eight-headed serpent Yamata no Orochi had eaten seven of their eight daughters. He set out eight barrels of strong sake. The serpent drank, fell asleep, and Susanoo cut off its heads.|The Kojiki||e|712|Susanoo, Amaterasu's unruly brother, saved the last daughter, Kushinada, and married her. In the serpent's tail he found a great sword, Kusanagi, which he gave to his sister in reconciliation. It became one of the three imperial regalia of Japan. The troublemaker of heaven becomes a hero on earth: the Kojiki shows even gods can change.
mythworld|The Moon Princess|An old bamboo cutter found a tiny girl inside a glowing stalk of bamboo. She grew into the beautiful Kaguya-hime, refused every suitor, even the emperor, and at last revealed she came from the Moon. On the full moon, her people came to take her home.|The Tale of the Bamboo Cutter||e|10th century|Often called Japan's oldest surviving story, the Taketori Monogatari sets five suitors impossible tasks, such as fetching a jewel from a dragon's neck. Before leaving, Kaguya-hime left the emperor a letter and the elixir of life, which he had burned on the highest mountain, closest to the sky. That, the tale says, is why smoke rises from Mount Fuji.
mythworld|The Crane's Gift|A poor man freed a crane caught in a trap. Soon after, a young woman came to his door and became his wife. She wove cloth of wondrous beauty, on one condition: he must never watch her at the loom. When he looked, he saw a crane plucking its own feathers.|Japanese folktale, Tsuru no Ongaeshi||e|Japan|Her secret discovered, the crane wife flew away and never returned. The tale, "the crane's repayment", is about gratitude that gives until it hurts, and about trust: love asked for one thing, and curiosity broke it. It inspired the play Yūzuru, an opera, and the Decemberists' songs "The Crane Wife".
mythworld|Hou Yi Shoots the Suns|Once ten suns rose together and scorched the earth. The archer Hou Yi shot down nine of them, leaving the one we have, so that crops could grow and people could live.|Chinese myth||e|ancient China|The ten suns were the children of the god Di Jun, who took turns crossing the sky, one each day, until they all rose at once. Hou Yi's arrows turned them into three-legged crows that fell to earth. He also killed monsters that plagued the land. The gods rewarded him with the elixir of immortality, which leads to the story of his wife, Chang'e.
mythworld|Chang'e Flies to the Moon|Chang'e, wife of the archer Hou Yi, drank the elixir of immortality and floated up to the Moon. There she lives in a cold palace, with only a jade rabbit for company, and every Mid-Autumn Festival people look up and think of her.|Chinese myth||e|ancient China|Versions differ: in some Chang'e drank the elixir to keep it from a thief, in others out of curiosity. Hou Yi, heartbroken, laid out her favourite fruits and cakes under the moon, the origin, some say, of mooncakes. China named its lunar exploration programme Chang'e, and its rovers Yutu, after the jade rabbit.
mythworld|Yu the Great Tames the Flood|When a great flood covered China, Yu's father tried to stop it with dams and failed. Yu worked for thirteen years digging channels to lead the water to the sea, and passed his own home three times without going in.|Chinese myth and legend||e|traditionally about 2000 BC|Yu's father, Gun, had stolen magic soil to build walls against the water, and the flood only grew worse. Yu learned from his failure: instead of fighting the water, he guided it. He became the founder of the Xia dynasty. Chinese tradition holds him up as the model of selfless public service, and "guide, don't block" is still a proverb for dealing with problems.
mythworld|The Jade Rabbit|Three animals, a monkey, a fox and a rabbit, wished to feed a hungry old man. The monkey brought fruit and the fox brought fish, but the rabbit had nothing, so it leapt into the fire to offer itself. The old man, a god in disguise, saved it and set it in the moon.|Buddhist Jataka tale and Chinese myth||e|India, then China and Japan|The story begins in the Jataka tales of the Buddha's past lives, where the rabbit is the Buddha himself. The god Shakra marked its image on the moon so that all would remember its generosity. In China, the jade rabbit pounds the elixir of life with a pestle; in Japan, it pounds rice cakes. Look at the full moon and you may see its ears.
mythworld|Anansi and the Pot of Wisdom|Anansi the spider gathered all the world's wisdom into a pot and tried to hide it at the top of a tree. He tied the pot in front of him and could not climb. His small son said: tie it on your back. Furious that a child was wiser, Anansi dropped the pot, and wisdom scattered over the world.|Akan folktale||e|Ghana and West Africa|Anansi, the clever and greedy spider, is the great trickster of Akan stories, which travelled with enslaved people to the Caribbean and the Americas, where he is still Anansi, Anancy or Aunt Nancy. This tale explains why no one owns all wisdom: it is shared in bits and pieces among everyone. It also carries a lesson Anansi rarely learns: listen to the young.
mythworld|Why the Sky Is Far Away|Long ago the sky was so close that people cut pieces from it to eat. The sky warned them not to waste it, but a greedy woman cut more than she could eat and threw it away. Angry, the sky rose high above the earth, and since then people have had to work for their food.|Bini folktale||e|Nigeria|This story comes from the Bini people of Benin in Nigeria. In the beginning the sky fed everyone freely, and no one needed to farm. Its warning was clear: take only what you need. One person's waste cost everyone the gift. Similar stories are told across West Africa, explaining the distance between people and the divine as the result of human carelessness.
mythworld|Obatala Shapes the People|Obatala came down from the sky on a golden chain and spread earth over the waters. Then he shaped people from clay. One day he drank palm wine and his hands grew unsteady, and some of the people he made were different. Sober again, he vowed to protect them always.|Yoruba myth||e|Nigeria and the Yoruba diaspora|Obatala is the orisha of purity, wisdom and creation in Yoruba religion, which spread to Cuba, Brazil and beyond. Because of his mistake, he became the special protector of disabled people, and his followers never drink palm wine. The myth is unusual: a god owns his error and turns it into lifelong care.
mythworld|Oshun Brings Back the Rain|When the world was made, the male orishas ignored Oshun, the orisha of sweet water and love. So she withdrew, and the rivers dried, the rain stopped and nothing grew. Only when the others asked her pardon did the waters flow again.|Yoruba myth||e|Nigeria and the Yoruba diaspora|In this tale, Olodumare, the supreme god, tells the orishas that nothing can succeed without Oshun.
