She is honoured at the Osun-Osogbo sacred grove in Nigeria, a World Heritage site, with a great festival each August. The story is about the quiet power of what is overlooked, and the sweetness without which life cannot go on.
mythworld|Sundiata|Sundiata Keita was born unable to walk, and mocked for it. One day, to defend his mother's honour, he pulled himself up on an iron rod so strong it bent, and stood. He grew into the king who founded the empire of Mali.|The Epic of Sundiata (Mande)||e|13th century, Mali|The epic is still sung by griots, the hereditary storytellers and musicians of West Africa. Exiled as a youth, Sundiata returned to defeat the sorcerer-king Sumanguru in about 1235. His empire became one of the richest in the world, later ruled by Mansa Musa. The story of the boy who could not walk is often said to have helped inspire The Lion King.
mythworld|Sky Woman|Sky Woman fell from a hole in the sky world towards an endless sea. Birds caught her on their wings, and animals dived to bring up mud, which they spread on the back of a great turtle. She danced on it, and it grew into the land we live on.|Haudenosaunee creation story||e|northeastern North America|This is the creation story of the Haudenosaunee (Iroquois) peoples. In many tellings the muskrat, smallest of the divers, gave its life to bring up the mud. That is why North America is called Turtle Island by many Indigenous peoples. Sky Woman planted seeds she carried from the sky world, and her descendants include the twins who shaped the world, good and difficult.
mythworld|Grandmother Spider Brings the Sun|When the world was dark, the animals sent Possum and then Buzzard to steal a piece of the sun. Possum burned his tail and Buzzard the feathers on his head. Then little Grandmother Spider wove a web, made a clay bowl, and carried a piece of the sun home safely.|Cherokee story||e|southeastern North America|The animals had heard that people on the other side of the world had the sun. The bold ones failed, and still bear the marks: the possum's bare tail, the buzzard's bald head. Grandmother Spider, old and small, succeeded by being careful and clever. She also brought fire and taught people to make pottery. Spider webs, the Cherokee say, still look like the sun's rays.
mythworld|The Three Sisters|Corn, beans and squash are three sisters who grow best together. The corn stands tall for the beans to climb, the beans feed the soil, and the squash spreads its leaves to keep the ground moist and the weeds away.|Haudenosaunee tradition||e|northeastern North America|The Three Sisters grew from the grave of Sky Woman's daughter, in some tellings, as gifts to the people. The story is also good farming: planted together in mounds, the three crops help each other and give a balanced diet. Indigenous peoples across North America grew them for centuries before Europeans arrived. They are a living image of different gifts held in partnership.
mythworld|The First Strawberries|The first man and woman quarrelled, and the woman walked away towards the sunrise. The Sun, taking pity on the man, put berries in her path, but she passed them by, until she saw ripe strawberries. She stopped to pick them, remembered her husband, and turned back to share them with him.|Cherokee story||e|southeastern North America|In this Cherokee story, the Sun tries huckleberries, blackberries and serviceberries before the strawberries, the first ever seen, slow the woman's anger. She gathers them and goes home, and the two are reconciled. Strawberries are still called the berries of love and forgiveness in Cherokee tradition. It is a myth about how sweetness can soften a quarrel.
mythworld|Rangi and Papa|In the beginning, Ranginui the sky father and Papatūānuku the earth mother lay locked in an embrace, and their children lived cramped in darkness between them. Tāne, god of the forest, pushed them apart with his feet, and light came into the world.|Māori creation story||e|Aotearoa New Zealand|Some of the children wanted to kill their parents; Tāne chose to separate them instead. Since then, Rangi's tears fall as rain and dew, and Papa's sighs rise as mist. Tāwhirimātea, god of the winds, who opposed the separation, still rages in storms. The story is about the pain of growing up and leaving, and the space that makes life possible.
mythworld|Māui Fishes Up the Land|Māui hid in his brothers' canoe and, far out at sea, cast a magic hook made from his grandmother's jawbone. He hooked a fish so vast it became the North Island of New Zealand, Te Ika-a-Māui, the fish of Māui.|Māori myth||e|Aotearoa New Zealand|Māui told his brothers not to touch the fish until he had made an offering to the gods, but they began cutting it up, which is why the land is so full of hills and valleys. The South Island is called his canoe, and Stewart Island its anchor stone. Māui, the clever demigod who also slowed the sun and brought fire, is a hero across Polynesia.
mythworld|Hina Goes to the Moon|Hina, tired of endless work and of her ungrateful family, paddled her canoe to the moon, or climbed a rainbow there, to live in peace. On clear nights you can see her beating out bark cloth, which drifts across the sky as clouds.|Polynesian myth||e|Hawaii, Tahiti and across Polynesia|Hina is one of the most widespread figures in Polynesian myth, a goddess of the moon and of women's work such as making kapa (bark cloth). In Hawaiian versions, her husband grabbed her leg as she went, and it broke off, so she limps across the sky. She is honoured as a woman who chose rest and her own path.
mythworld|Pele's Fire|Pele, goddess of fire and volcanoes, travelled from Tahiti across the ocean, digging fire pits on each Hawaiian island, until she found her home in the crater of Kīlauea on the Big Island, where she lives still.|Hawaiian myth||e|Hawaii|Pele was chased from island to island by her sister Nāmakaokahaʻi, goddess of the sea, who flooded each pit she dug. The order of her journey follows the islands' real geological ages, oldest to youngest. Hawaiians still leave offerings at the crater's edge, and say that taking lava rocks home brings bad luck. Fire and water, creation and destruction, shape the islands together.
mythworld|The Hero Twins|The twins Hunahpu and Xbalanque were summoned to Xibalba, the Maya underworld, to play the ball game against its lords. They passed every trial by their wits, let themselves be killed and rose again, and defeated the lords of death. Then they rose into the sky as the sun and the moon.|Popol Vuh (K'iche' Maya)||e|written down in the 16th century|Their father and uncle had been killed in Xibalba before them. The twins survived the House of Gloom, the House of Knives, the House of Cold, the House of Jaguars, the House of Fire and the House of Bats. Their victory made way for the creation of humans from maize. The Popol Vuh, the K'iche' Maya book of counsel, was written down in the 1550s in Latin letters to keep it alive.
mythworld|The Eagle on the Cactus|The Mexica wandered for generations, looking for the sign their god had promised: an eagle perched on a cactus, devouring a serpent. When they saw it on an island in Lake Texcoco, they founded their city there, Tenochtitlan, today Mexico City.|Aztec legend||e|founded 1325|The god Huitzilopochtli had told the wandering Mexica where to settle. The island was swampy and unpromising, but they built causeways, canals and floating gardens, and their city became one of the largest in the world. The eagle on the cactus is at the centre of Mexico's flag to this day.
mythworld|Coyolxauhqui and the Moon|When the earth goddess Coatlicue became pregnant, her daughter Coyolxauhqui and her four hundred brothers plotted to kill her. But the war god Huitzilopochtli sprang from his mother fully armed, defeated them, and threw Coyolxauhqui's head into the sky, where it became the moon.|Aztec myth||e|Mexico|The four hundred brothers became the stars of the southern sky. The Aztecs saw the story replayed every dawn, as the sun rises and drives away the moon and stars. In 1978, workers in Mexico City found a huge round stone carving of Coyolxauhqui, which led to the excavation of the Templo Mayor, the great temple of Tenochtitlan.
mythworld|Mayahuel and the Agave|Mayahuel, a beautiful young goddess, fled the sky with the wind god Quetzalcoatl. Her fierce grandmother found and killed her, and Quetzalcoatl, grieving, buried her bones. From them grew the first agave plant.|Aztec myth||e|Mexico|From the agave's sap, the Aztecs made pulque, a sacred drink offered to the gods. Mayahuel became the goddess of the agave, often shown with four hundred breasts to feed her four hundred rabbit children, the gods of drunkenness. Like many myths, it finds new life growing from loss.
mythworld|The Firebird|Ivan, the youngest son, set out to catch the Firebird, whose glowing feathers stole golden apples from the tsar's garden. With the help of a grey wolf he caught the bird, and won a princess too.|Russian folktale||e|Russia|The Firebird, Zhar-ptitsa, shines so bright that a single feather can light a room. In the tale, the grey wolf saves Ivan again and again, even bringing him back to life with the water of life. Stravinsky's ballet The Firebird (1910), made for Diaghilev's Ballets Russes, made the story famous worldwide. The bird is a symbol of a wonderful, hard-won goal.
mythworld|The Sampo|The smith Ilmarinen forged the Sampo, a magic mill that ground out flour, salt and gold, to win the daughter of Louhi, mistress of the North. Later the heroes stole it back, and in the struggle at sea it broke, and its pieces brought good fortune to Finland.|The Kalevala||e|1835 (from older Finnish songs)|No one is quite sure what the Sampo was: a mill, a world pillar, the vault of the sky. Ilmarinen forged it from a swan's feather, the milk of a barren cow, a grain of barley and the wool of a summer ewe. Its fragments washing ashore explain the land's fertility. The Kalevala helped shape Finland's sense of itself as a nation, and Tolkien loved it.
mythworld|Lemminkäinen's Mother|The reckless hero Lemminkäinen was killed and his body cut to pieces in the river of Tuonela, the land of the dead. His mother raked the river with a giant rake until she found every piece, sewed him together, and with a bee's help brought him back to life.|The Kalevala||e|1835 (from older Finnish songs)|The scene is one of the most moving in the Kalevala. The bee flies over the heavens to bring back honey from the highest god, and the ointment heals her son. The painter Akseli Gallen-Kallela painted the mother bending over her son, and Sibelius wrote a suite for Lemminkäinen. It is a myth about a mother's love that will not give up.
mythworld|Gesar of Ling|Gesar was sent from heaven as a child into a land overrun by demons. Born to a poor family and scorned as a boy, he won a horse race to become king of Ling, and spent his life fighting the demons that brought suffering.|Tibetan epic||e|Tibet, Mongolia and Central Asia, from about the 12th century|The Epic of King Gesar is perhaps the longest epic in the world, with over a hundred volumes, still sung by bards who say the verses come to them in dreams. Gesar is a warrior king, but also a Buddhist hero whose battles are against greed, hatred and ignorance. UNESCO lists the Gesar tradition as intangible cultural heritage.
ideamath|The Taxicab Number|1729 is the smallest number that can be written as the sum of two cubes in two different ways: 1³ + 12³ and 9³ + 10³.|Srinivasa Ramanujan||e|1918|When G. H. Hardy visited Ramanujan in hospital, he remarked that his taxi's number, 1729, seemed a dull one. No, Ramanujan answered at once: it is a very interesting number. Self-taught in India, Ramanujan had sent Hardy pages of astonishing formulas in 1913, and Hardy brought him to Cambridge. He died at 32, leaving notebooks mathematicians are still working through. Numbers like 1729 are now called taxicab numbers.
ideamath|Ramanujan's Formulas for Pi|Ramanujan found infinite series that close in on π astonishingly fast, each new term adding about eight correct digits.|Srinivasa Ramanujan||e|1914|Ramanujan published seventeen series for 1/π in 1914, with little explanation of how he found them; he said his insights came to him from the goddess Namagiri in dreams. Decades later his formulas, and others built on them, became the basis of computer programs that calculate π to billions and trillions of digits. His work shows how far intuition can run ahead of proof.
ideamath|Hypatia of Alexandria|Hypatia taught mathematics, astronomy and philosophy in Alexandria, writing commentaries on Diophantus' algebra and Apollonius' conic sections, and was the most celebrated woman scholar of the ancient world.|Hypatia||e|about 400|Daughter of the mathematician Theon, Hypatia edited and taught the great mathematical works of her day, and students came from far away to hear her. In 415 she was murdered by a Christian mob amid political strife in Alexandria. None of her own writings survive intact, but she became a lasting symbol of learning, reason and the courage to teach.
ideamath|Al-Jabr: Restoring the Balance|Al-Khwarizmi showed how to solve equations step by step, by "restoring" and "balancing" both sides. His word al-jabr gave us algebra, and his name gave us algorithm.|Muhammad ibn Musa al-Khwarizmi||e|about 820|Working at the House of Wisdom in Baghdad, al-Khwarizmi wrote The Compendious Book on Calculation by Completion and Balancing. It solved practical problems of inheritance, trade and land measurement, with methods that work for any numbers, not just one case. Translated into Latin, it taught Europe algebra. His name, Latinised as Algoritmi, became "algorithm": a recipe that always works.
ideamath|Hindu–Arabic Numerals|The ten digits 0 to 9, where a digit's place gives its value, make arithmetic easy. They were invented in India, spread by Arab scholars, and reached Europe through Fibonacci.|Indian mathematicians; al-Khwarizmi; Fibonacci||e|5th–13th centuries|Indian mathematicians had a place-value system with zero by about the 5th century. Al-Khwarizmi wrote a book on Indian arithmetic in Baghdad, and in 1202 Leonardo of Pisa, known as Fibonacci, introduced the numerals to Europe in his Liber Abaci, having learned them as a boy in North Africa. Try multiplying in Roman numerals to feel the difference.
ideamath|Thinking in Structures|Emmy Noether taught mathematicians to look past numbers and shapes to the structures beneath them: the rules by which things combine. Modern abstract algebra grew from her work.|Emmy Noether||e|1920s|At Göttingen, Noether was at first not allowed to lecture under her own name because she was a woman, and taught under David Hilbert's. Her work on rings and ideals changed algebra completely; her students were known as "Noether's boys". Forced out by the Nazis in 1933, she went to Bryn Mawr in America. Einstein called her the most significant creative mathematical genius since women began higher education.
ideamath|Note G|Ada Lovelace wrote what is often called the first computer program, for a machine that was never built, and saw that it might one day handle not just numbers but music and symbols of any kind.|Ada Lovelace||e|1843|Lovelace translated an article on Charles Babbage's Analytical Engine and added notes three times longer than the original. Note G set out step by step how the engine could compute Bernoulli numbers. She also argued it could compose music if the rules of harmony were expressed in its terms, though it could only do what we told it, never originate anything. A century later, Turing answered her.
ideamath|Monsieur LeBlanc|Barred from the École Polytechnique because she was a woman, Sophie Germain studied from borrowed lecture notes and sent her work to the great Gauss under a man's name, Monsieur LeBlanc.|Sophie Germain||e|1804|When Gauss discovered who LeBlanc really was, he wrote that she must have the noblest courage and extraordinary talent to have overcome such obstacles. Germain made the first real progress on Fermat's Last Theorem in a century, and won the Paris Academy's prize for her theory of vibrating surfaces. Germain primes, such as 11 (where 2 × 11 + 1 = 23 is also prime), bear her name.
ideamath|Gauss's Schoolroom Shortcut|Told to add up the numbers from 1 to 100, the young Gauss saw at once that they form fifty pairs each adding to 101 (1 + 100, 2 + 99…), so the answer is 5,050.|Carl Friedrich Gauss||e|about 1786|The story may have grown in the telling, but the trick is real and lovely: the sum of 1 to n is n × (n + 1) ÷ 2. Gauss went on to become one of the greatest mathematicians of all time, the "prince of mathematicians". The story is loved because it shows what mathematics is: not grinding through, but seeing a pattern that makes the work disappear.
ideamath|The Kerala School|In 14th-century Kerala, Madhava of Sangamagrama found infinite series for π, sine and cosine, ideas of calculus that Europe would reach only three centuries later.|Madhava of Sangamagrama||e|14th century|Madhava founded a line of astronomer-mathematicians in southern India that lasted two hundred years. His results survive in the works of his followers, such as Jyeṣṭhadeva's Yuktibhāṣā, written in Malayalam. The series π/4 = 1 − 1/3 + 1/5 − 1/7… is often called the Madhava–Leibniz series. The history of mathematics is wider than the story told in Europe.
ideamath|Zu Chongzhi's Pi|In 5th-century China, Zu Chongzhi calculated π to seven decimal places, between 3.1415926 and 3.1415927, and found the fraction 355/113, which stayed the most accurate value in the world for nearly a thousand years.|Zu Chongzhi||e|about 480|Zu built on Liu Hui's method of fitting polygons with more and more sides inside a circle, probably going to over twelve thousand sides by hand. The fraction 355/113 is easy to remember (one-one, three-three, five-five, split in the middle) and correct to six decimal places. Zu was also an astronomer and engineer who designed a calendar and a south-pointing chariot.
ideamath|Khayyam's Cubic Equations|The poet of the Rubáiyát was also a great mathematician, who solved cubic equations with the geometry of intersecting curves.|Omar Khayyam||e|about 1070|In his Treatise on Demonstration of Problems of Algebra, Khayyam classified the kinds of cubic equation and solved each by intersecting a circle with a parabola or hyperbola. He also reformed the Persian calendar, making it more accurate than the Gregorian one adopted in Europe centuries later. The same mind wrote quatrains about wine, roses and the brevity of life.
ideamath|Euler in the Dark|Leonhard Euler lost the sight of one eye, then went almost completely blind, and still produced about half of his life's work afterwards, dictating it from memory.|Leonhard Euler||e|1770s|Euler is the most prolific mathematician in history: his collected works fill over seventy volumes. When he became blind, he said it meant fewer distractions. He did huge calculations in his head and dictated papers to his sons and assistants. He gave us the notation f(x), e, i and the use of π. His story is one of how a mind can find a way around loss.
ideamath|Galois's Last Night|Évariste Galois, aged twenty, spent the night before a fatal duel writing out his ideas in a letter to a friend. They became group theory, one of the great foundations of modern mathematics.|Évariste Galois||e|1832|Galois found the reason why there is no general formula for equations of the fifth degree: it lies in the symmetries of their solutions, which he called groups. His papers had been lost or rejected by the Academy. In his last letter he asked that his work be shown to Gauss and Jacobi, "not for the truth, but for the importance". It was published fourteen years later, and transformed mathematics.
ideamath|Nightingale's Rose|Florence Nightingale drew a new kind of chart, a "rose" of coloured wedges, to show that far more soldiers in the Crimean War were dying of preventable disease than of wounds. It persuaded the government to reform army hospitals.|Florence Nightingale||e|1858|Nightingale is remembered as the lady with the lamp, but she was also a pioneering statistician, the first woman elected to the Royal Statistical Society. She knew that numbers alone did not move politicians, so she made them visible. Her diagrams helped change sanitation in hospitals and cities. Data, she showed, can be an act of compassion.
ideamath|Katherine Johnson's Trajectories|Before John Glenn would fly around the Earth in 1962, he asked for the computer's numbers to be checked by hand by one person: Katherine Johnson. "If she says they're good," he said, "then I'm ready to go."|Katherine Johnson||e|1962|Johnson was one of the Black women "computers" at NASA's Langley centre, working in segregated offices. Her calculations of orbits and trajectories helped send Alan Shepard into space and Apollo 11 to the Moon. Her story, told in the film Hidden Figures, came to wide attention only late in her life. In 2015 she received the Presidential Medal of Freedom.
ideamath|Can Machines Think?|Alan Turing proposed a test: if a machine can hold a conversation so well that we cannot tell it from a person, we should grant that it thinks.|Alan Turing||e|1950|In "Computing Machinery and Intelligence", Turing replaced the vague question with "the imitation game", and answered objections one by one, including Ada Lovelace's, that machines can only do what we tell them. He predicted thinking machines would be widely accepted by the end of the century. Turing had helped break the Enigma code in the war, and was persecuted for being gay; he died in 1954.
ideamath|Erdős Numbers|Paul Erdős wrote about 1,500 papers with over 500 collaborators. Mathematicians measure their closeness to him: co-authors have Erdős number 1, their co-authors 2, and so on.|Paul Erdős||e|20th century|Erdős had no home and few possessions. He travelled from one mathematician's house to the next with a suitcase, announcing "My brain is open!" and working with his hosts for days. He called children "epsilons" and God "the Supreme Fascist" who kept the best proofs in "The Book". He showed that mathematics, often imagined as solitary, can be deeply social.
ideamath|Mirzakhani's Doodles|Maryam Mirzakhani worked on huge sheets of paper on the floor, drawing pictures of curved surfaces. In 2014 she became the first woman to win the Fields Medal, mathematics' highest honour.|Maryam Mirzakhani||e|2014|Growing up in Tehran, Mirzakhani wanted to be a writer; she liked mathematics only later. She studied the geometry of curved surfaces and how paths wind across them, like billiard balls on strange tables. Her young daughter called her work "painting". She died of cancer at forty. "The beauty of mathematics only shows itself to more patient followers," she said.
ideamath|The First Compiler|Grace Hopper believed computers should understand something like ordinary language, not just numbers. Her compiler translated human-readable instructions into machine code, and led to COBOL.|Grace Hopper||e|1952|Told that computers could only do arithmetic, Hopper built one anyway that read words. A rear admiral in the US Navy, she popularised the term "bug" after a moth was found in a relay. She handed out pieces of wire 30 centimetres long to show how far light travels in a nanosecond. Her motto: it is easier to ask forgiveness than to get permission.
ideamath|The Game of Life|On a grid of squares, each cell lives or dies by a few simple rules about its neighbours. From those rules come gliders, guns and patterns that grow forever: complexity from almost nothing.|John Horton Conway||e|1970|Conway invented the Game of Life with pencil and paper; it became famous through Martin Gardner's column in Scientific American. A live cell survives with two or three live neighbours; a dead cell comes alive with exactly three. Remarkably, the game can carry out any computation. It became a picture of how life and mind might arise from simple parts.
ideamath|The Sand Reckoner|Archimedes set out to count how many grains of sand it would take to fill the whole universe, and invented a way of naming numbers far beyond anything the Greeks had words for.|Archimedes||e|about 230 BC|People said the grains of sand were beyond number. Archimedes showed they were not: by his estimate of the cosmos, about 10⁶³ grains would fill it. To do so he built a system of powers of a myriad myriad (100 million). In the same work he mentions Aristarchus's idea that the Earth goes round the Sun. Even the uncountable, he showed, can be counted.
ideamath|Eratosthenes Measures the Earth|Eratosthenes noticed that at noon on midsummer's day the Sun shone straight down a well at Syene, but cast a shadow at Alexandria. From the angle and the distance between the cities, he worked out the size of the whole Earth.|Eratosthenes||e|about 240 BC|The shadow's angle was about one fiftieth of a circle, so the Earth's circumference was fifty times the distance from Alexandria to Syene. His answer, around 250,000 stadia, was remarkably close to the true figure. Eratosthenes, chief librarian at Alexandria, also mapped the world and found prime numbers with his "sieve". A stick, a shadow and a good question measured a planet.
ideamath|Yang Hui's Triangle|Write 1, then build each row by adding the two numbers above: 1, 1 1, 1 2 1, 1 3 3 1… The triangle holds counting, chance and the powers of sums.|Yang Hui; Blaise Pascal||e|1261 and 1654|Known in Europe as Pascal's triangle, it was described centuries earlier in India by Pingala's followers, in Persia by al-Karaji and Omar Khayyam, and in China by Jia Xian and Yang Hui, after whom it is named there. Its rows give the number of ways to choose things, the odds of coin tosses, and the expansion of (a + b)ⁿ. Shade the odd numbers and you see a fractal.
ideamath|Binary|Every number can be written with only two digits, 0 and 1. Leibniz saw in this a picture of creation from nothing and one; today it is the language of every computer.|Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz||e|1703|Leibniz published his "Explanation of Binary Arithmetic" after a Jesuit missionary in China sent him the hexagrams of the I Ching, whose broken and unbroken lines he saw as the same system. Two and a half centuries later, Claude Shannon showed that switching circuits could carry out binary logic, and the digital age began. Everything on your screen is, deep down, ones and zeros.
ideaart|Chiaroscuro|Light and dark set side by side: figures emerge from shadow as if lit by a single lamp, giving paintings depth, drama and weight.|Leonardo da Vinci; Caravaggio||e|15th–17th centuries|The Italian word means "light-dark". Leonardo studied how light falls on round forms; Caravaggio pushed it to extremes, painting saints and gamblers in pitch-dark rooms cut by a beam of light, a style called tenebrism. Rembrandt and Georges de La Tour made it tender. Film-makers and photographers still use it: what we leave in shadow shapes what we see.
ideaart|Sfumato|Leonardo softened every outline into smoke, so that edges melt gently into shadow. It is why the Mona Lisa's smile seems to change as you look.|Leonardo da Vinci||e|about 1503|Sfumato comes from the Italian for smoke. Leonardo built up dozens of translucent layers of glaze, some barely thicker than a breath, so that no line is ever hard. He noticed that in nature there are no outlines, only shifting tones. The corners of the Mona Lisa's mouth and eyes are left in soft shadow, so our minds fill them in differently from moment to moment.
ideaart|Linear Perspective|Lines running away from us meet at a single vanishing point on the horizon. With this rule, painters could make a flat surface open like a window into space.|Filippo Brunelleschi; Leon Battista Alberti||e|about 1415|Brunelleschi demonstrated it with a painted panel of the Florence Baptistery, viewed through a hole in its back and reflected in a mirror; Alberti wrote down the rules in On Painting (1435). It changed Western art, and how people pictured the world, as seen from one fixed eye. Other traditions, such as Chinese scroll painting, chose many viewpoints instead.
ideaart|Impression|Paint what the eye actually sees in a moment, the flicker of light on water, not what the mind knows is there. A critic mocked Monet's Impression, Sunrise, and the name stuck.|Claude Monet and the Impressionists||e|1872–1874|Working outdoors with new paint in tubes, the Impressionists caught light and weather with quick, broken brushstrokes. Rejected by the official Salon, they held their own exhibition in 1874. Monet later painted the same haystacks, poplars and cathedral front again and again at different hours, to show that light, not the object, was his true subject.
ideaart|Optical Mixing|Instead of mixing colours on the palette, Seurat set tiny dots of pure colour side by side, and let the viewer's eye blend them, so the colours stay brighter.|Georges Seurat||e|1884–1886|Seurat read the colour science of Michel Chevreul and Ogden Rood and spent two years on A Sunday Afternoon on the Island of La Grande Jatte, built from thousands of dots. The style was called pointillism. The same principle works in every screen you look at, where red, green and blue points mix in your eye into every colour.
ideaart|Cubism: Many Views at Once|Picasso and Braque broke objects into facets and showed them from several sides at once, as we really know things: not from one fixed point, but by moving around them.|Pablo Picasso; Georges Braque||e|1907–1914|Picasso's Les Demoiselles d'Avignon (1907) shocked even his friends. With Braque, working so closely they called themselves roped mountaineers, he took apart the single viewpoint perspective had ruled for five centuries. They later stuck newspaper and wallpaper onto canvases, inventing collage. Cubism opened the door to abstract art.
ideaart|Surrealism|Art should free the unconscious: dreams, chance and strange juxtapositions reveal a truer reality, a sur-reality, beneath everyday life.|André Breton; Salvador Dalí; René Magritte||e|1924|Breton's Surrealist Manifesto drew on Freud: the mind's hidden workings should speak without the control of reason. Surrealists tried automatic writing, games of chance and painting dream images: Dalí's melting clocks, Magritte's pipe that is "not a pipe". The movement gave everyday language the word "surreal" for anything as strange as a dream.
ideaart|The Black Square|In 1915 Malevich hung a painting of a black square on a white ground high in the corner of the room, where an icon would hang in a Russian home: the "zero of form", art freed from depicting anything.|Kazimir Malevich||e|1915|Malevich called his movement Suprematism, the supremacy of pure feeling in art. The Black Square was meant as a new beginning, an end to painting things and a start on painting itself. It was mocked, revered and later hidden by Soviet authorities. Under the cracked black paint, X-rays have found earlier compositions, and a pencilled inscription Malevich added in the margin.
ideaart|Pictures of the Floating World|Ukiyo-e, "pictures of the floating world", showed the pleasures of everyday Edo: actors, courtesans, travellers and landscapes, printed in colour from woodblocks for ordinary people to buy.|Hokusai; Hiroshige; Utamaro||e|17th–19th centuries|The "floating world" was a Buddhist phrase for life's sorrows, turned into a celebration of passing pleasures. Hokusai's Great Wave and Hiroshige's views of the road to Edo were cheap prints. When they reached Europe, packed around exported porcelain, they astonished Monet, Degas and van Gogh, who copied them; their flat colours and bold cropping helped shape modern art.
ideaart|The Cutting Word|A Japanese haiku often turns on a kireji, a "cutting word" that splits the poem in two, so that two images spark against each other and the reader leaps the gap.|Matsuo Bashō and the haiku poets||e|17th century|In Bashō's famous poem, "old pond" is followed by the cutting word ya, and then: "a frog jumps in, the sound of water." The silence of the pond and the sudden splash meet across the cut. English has no exact equivalent; translators use a dash or a colon. The cut is where the poem happens: in what is left unsaid between the two parts.
ideaart|The Sonnet's Turn|Fourteen lines with a turn, the volta: after setting out a problem or feeling, the sonnet suddenly shifts, answers or deepens it.|Petrarch; Shakespeare||e|13th–16th centuries|The sonnet, "little song", was born in Sicily and perfected by Petrarch, whose turn comes after eight lines. Shakespeare saved it for a final rhyming couplet. Poets from Milton to Wordsworth, Elizabeth Barrett Browning to Claude McKay, have used its small room to hold great feelings. The form's power lies in that turn: a thought changing its mind.
ideaart|The Leitmotif|A short musical theme tied to a character, an object or an idea, which returns, changing as the story unfolds, so the music tells us what the words do not.|Richard Wagner||e|1850s–1870s|Wagner wove dozens of leitmotifs through his four-opera Ring cycle: for the gold, the sword, the curse, Valhalla. When a motif returns in the orchestra while a character says something else, we hear what they really mean or have forgotten. Film composers, from Max Steiner to John Williams with Darth Vader's march, have used the idea ever since.
ideaart|The Fugue|One melody enters alone, then another voice takes it up while the first continues, then another, until several independent lines weave together, each whole in itself, yet all one.|Johann Sebastian Bach||e|early 18th century|The fugue, from the Latin for flight, as voices seem to chase each other, reached its height with Bach, in The Well-Tempered Clavier and The Art of Fugue, left unfinished at his death. Its order is strict, yet the effect can be ecstatic. Douglas Hofstadter's Gödel, Escher, Bach used the fugue as a picture of minds and meaning built from self-reference.
ideaart|Sonata Form|A musical drama in three acts: themes are set out, then broken up, developed and put in conflict, and finally return, changed by the journey and brought home.|Haydn, Mozart and Beethoven||e|18th century|Sonata form shaped the first movements of countless symphonies, sonatas and quartets: exposition, development, recapitulation. It works like a story, with a contrast of keys that must be resolved. Beethoven stretched it until it almost broke, making his developments vast battles. The form shows how music, without words, can have a plot.
ideaart|Twelve Tones|Schoenberg gave all twelve notes of the scale equal weight, arranged in a row, so that no note is "home". Music left the pull of a key behind.|Arnold Schoenberg||e|1921|For centuries Western music had a tonal centre, the note where a piece comes to rest. Schoenberg's twelve-tone method, which he said would ensure the supremacy of German music for a hundred years, created a new kind of order without it. Listeners often found it hard, but it changed modern music, film scores for suspense among them.
ideaart|Phasing|Two players repeat the same short pattern, one very slightly faster, so that the patterns slowly drift apart and together, and new melodies appear out of the overlap.|Steve Reich||e|1965–1967|Reich discovered phasing by accident, when two tape loops of a street preacher's voice, played on two machines, slowly slipped out of sync. He then wrote it for live players, in Piano Phase and Clapping Music. With Philip Glass, Terry Riley and La Monte Young, he founded minimalism: music of repetition and slow change that invites a different, meditative kind of listening.
ideaart|The Twelve-Bar Blues|Three chords over twelve bars, a line sung, repeated, then answered: one of the simplest forms in music, and the root of jazz, rock and roll and much of popular song.|African American musicians of the Deep South||e|late 19th–early 20th century|The blues grew from work songs, spirituals and field hollers. In the classic form, the singer states a trouble twice and then turns it with a third line, often with wry humour. W. C. Handy, Bessie Smith, Robert Johnson and countless others made it a way of singing through hardship rather than hiding it. Nearly every rock guitarist learns it first.
ideaart|Montage and the Kuleshov Effect|The same shot of an actor's face, cut next to a bowl of soup, a child in a coffin or a woman on a sofa, seemed to show hunger, grief or desire. Meaning lives between the shots.|Lev Kuleshov; Sergei Eisenstein||e|1910s–1920s|Kuleshov's experiment showed that audiences read emotion into a neutral face from what came before and after. Eisenstein built a whole theory of montage on it, colliding images to create ideas, as in the Odessa Steps sequence of Battleship Potemkin (1925). Every film and video edit since works on the same discovery: we make the story in the cut.
ideaart|Breaking the Fourth Wall|Brecht wanted audiences not to lose themselves in a play, but to watch it critically: actors step out of the story, songs interrupt, signs announce what will happen.|Bertolt Brecht||e|1930s|The "fourth wall" is the invisible wall between stage and audience. Brecht called his technique the Verfremdungseffekt, the estrangement effect: by reminding us that we are watching a play, he hoped to make us think about the world it showed and how it could be changed. Comedians, novelists and television shows have all borrowed the trick of speaking straight to us.
ideaart|Magical Realism|The miraculous is told in the same calm voice as the everyday: a girl rises to heaven while folding sheets, it rains for four years, and no one is astonished.|Gabriel García Márquez; Alejo Carpentier||e|1940s–1960s|The term was first used by a German art critic, then taken up in Latin America, where Carpentier spoke of "the marvellous real" of a continent where history itself seemed fantastic. García Márquez said he learned the tone from his grandmother, who told the most unbelievable things with a straight face. It became a way of telling truths that plain realism could not hold.
ideaart|Free Indirect Style|A narrator slips into a character's mind without saying so, and the words take on the character's tone: we hear their thoughts and the author's irony at once.|Jane Austen; Gustave Flaubert||e|early 19th century|"Emma Woodhouse, handsome, clever, and rich" sounds partly like Emma's own opinion of herself. Austen mastered this blending of voices; Flaubert made it the heart of Madame Bovary. It lets novels show the inside of a mind while keeping a little distance. Much of what we feel about fictional people comes from this quiet, double voice.
ideaart|Jo-ha-kyū|Begin slowly, break open, and end swiftly: the Japanese rhythm of jo-ha-kyū shapes a Noh play, a tea ceremony, a single gesture and a whole day's programme.|Zeami Motokiyo||e|about 1400|Zeami, the great master of Noh theatre, wrote that everything in the universe, even a bird's song, follows jo, ha and kyū: introduction, development, rapid climax. A Noh performance moves through it in each scene and over the day. It is found in gagaku court music, martial arts and storytelling, a sense of natural tempo that does not hurry the opening or drag the end.
ideaart|Contrapposto|A standing figure rests its weight on one leg: hips tilt one way, shoulders the other, and the stone seems to breathe and be about to move.|Polykleitos and the Greek sculptors||e|5th century BC|Earlier Greek statues stood stiffly, weight on both feet. Around 480 BC sculptors discovered this relaxed, counterbalanced pose, as in the Kritios Boy. Polykleitos wrote the Canon, a book on perfect proportions, now lost. Michelangelo's David uses contrapposto. A small shift of weight turned stone into something alive.
ideaart|Islamic Geometric Pattern|With a compass and a straight edge, artists across the Islamic world built endlessly repeating stars and polygons, an image of an infinite, ordered creation with no beginning or end.|Islamic artists and architects||e|10th–17th centuries|The patterns cover the walls of the Alhambra in Granada, the mosques of Isfahan and the tombs of Bukhara. Many grow from simple circles and divisions, yet some, the girih tiles of 15th-century Iran, approach mathematical structures (quasicrystals) that Western science described only in the 1970s. Their endless repetition invites contemplation rather than a single focal point.
ideaart|The Sand Mandala|Tibetan monks spend days laying coloured sand grain by grain into a vast, intricate mandala, and then, when it is finished, sweep it away and pour the sand into a river.|Tibetan Buddhist tradition||e|living tradition|A mandala is a map of an enlightened universe, made as a meditation. Several monks work together, tapping sand from metal funnels. The ceremony of dissolution teaches impermanence: nothing made lasts, and the making itself is the point. The sand, carrying blessings, is given to flowing water to spread them through the world.
ideaart|Shan Shui|Chinese "mountain-water" painting does not copy a single view, but gathers mountains, mist, rivers and tiny travellers into a world to wander through with the eye, from many viewpoints.|Chinese landscape painters||e|10th century onwards|Painters such as Fan Kuan and Guo Xi painted towering mountains in ink, with paths and huts so small you must look closely to find them. Guo Xi wrote that a good landscape should be one you can walk in, gaze at, roam and live in. Empty mist is as important as the brushstrokes. The human is small, and at home, inside nature.
ideamind|Cognitive Defusion|A thought is not a fact, and not an order. Saying "I'm having the thought that I'm a failure" instead of "I'm a failure" puts a little space between you and the thought.|Steven C. Hayes, Acceptance and Commitment Therapy||e|1980s–1990s|Hayes found that arguing with painful thoughts often gives them more power. Defusion techniques, such as repeating a word until it becomes just a sound, or imagining thoughts as leaves on a stream, help us notice thoughts as passing events in the mind. They do not need to be believed, obeyed or got rid of. We can then act on what we care about, with the thoughts along for the ride.
ideamind|The Window of Tolerance|Each of us has a zone in which we can feel strong emotions and still think clearly. Above it lies panic and rage; below it, numbness and shutdown. Calm practices widen the window.|Daniel J. Siegel||e|1999|Siegel coined the term to describe the range of arousal in which we function well. Stress and trauma can narrow it, so that small things tip us into fight-or-flight, or into a frozen, flat state. Slow breathing, grounding through the senses and feeling safe with others can bring us back inside, and over time make the window wider.
ideamind|Urge Surfing|Cravings and urges rise, peak and fall like waves, usually within about half an hour. Instead of fighting them or giving in, you can ride them, noticing each sensation as it builds and passes.|G. Alan Marlatt||e|1980s|Marlatt developed urge surfing to help people recovering from addiction. The idea is that resisting an urge often makes it stronger, while observing it with curiosity lets it run its course. Each time we surf an urge without acting on it, it tends to grow weaker. The same approach can help with anger, panic or the pull to check a phone.
ideamind|Behavioural Activation|When we are low, we wait to feel like doing things before we do them. But action often comes first and motivation follows: small steps towards what matters can lift the mood.|Peter Lewinsohn; Neil Jacobson||e|1970s–1990s|Depression narrows life: we withdraw, stop doing what we enjoy, and feel worse, a downward spiral. Behavioural activation reverses it by planning small, doable activities that bring pleasure or a sense of mastery, whatever the mood. Studies found it works about as well as fuller cognitive therapy for many people. Outside in comes before inside out.
ideamind|Expressive Writing|Writing about our deepest thoughts and feelings on a painful experience, for fifteen to twenty minutes on three or four days, can improve health and wellbeing.|James W. Pennebaker||e|1986|In Pennebaker's first studies, students who wrote about traumatic experiences visited the health centre less often in the following months than those who wrote about trivial topics. Putting experience into words seems to help us make sense of it and let it rest. The benefits were greatest for people who moved from raw feeling towards understanding as they wrote.
ideamind|Psychological Safety|Teams do their best work when people feel safe to ask questions, admit mistakes and offer half-formed ideas without fear of being embarrassed or punished.|Amy Edmondson||e|1999|Studying hospital teams, Edmondson was surprised to find the best teams reported more errors, not fewer. They were not making more; they felt safe to speak about them, and so learned from them. Google's study of its own teams later found psychological safety mattered more than anything else. It is built by leaders who admit their own fallibility and welcome questions.
ideamind|The Doorway Effect|Walking through a doorway makes us more likely to forget what we came into the room for. The mind files memories into episodes, and a doorway marks the end of one.|Gabriel Radvansky||e|2011|In experiments, people who walked through a door forgot more than those who walked the same distance within a room, even in virtual reality. Doorways act as event boundaries: the mind closes one scene and opens another, making the plan formed in the last room harder to reach. Going back to where you started often brings the memory back.
ideasoc|The Peter Principle|In a hierarchy, people tend to be promoted until they reach a job they are no good at, and there they stay.|Laurence J. Peter||e|1969|Peter and Raymond Hull's book was half joke, half serious. If people are promoted for doing their current job well, they keep rising until they land in a role that needs different skills, where they stop being promoted. Over time, the theory says, every post tends to be filled by someone not up to it. Some organisations answer by letting people grow in skill without becoming managers.
ideasoc|The Lindy Effect|For things that do not age, such as books, ideas and technologies, the longer something has already lasted, the longer it is likely to last.|Albert Goldman; Benoit Mandelbrot; Nassim Nicholas Taleb||e|1964 onwards|The name comes from Lindy's delicatessen in New York, where comedians joked about which shows would last. A book still read after two thousand years, like Homer, is likely to be read for a long time yet; this year's bestseller may vanish. It is a rule of thumb for what to read: time is a good filter.
ideasoc|The Cobra Effect|A fix can make a problem worse. A bounty paid for dead cobras, the story goes, led people to breed cobras for the reward; when it was scrapped, they released them.|Horst Siebert||e|2001 (from a colonial story)|The economist Horst Siebert named the effect after an anecdote from British India, which may be more legend than history; a similar episode with rats in Hanoi under French rule is documented. Any reward that measures the wrong thing invites people to game it. Good intentions need to be checked against how people will actually respond.
ideasoc|Braess's Paradox|Adding a new road to a network can make traffic slower for everyone, because each driver choosing the quickest route for themselves can make all routes worse.|Dietrich Braess||e|1968|Braess, a German mathematician, showed this with a simple network model. It has happened in real cities: when Seoul removed a motorway to restore the Cheonggyecheon stream in 2005, traffic flowed better. The same paradox appears in electrical circuits and springs. What is best for each, chosen separately, is not always best for all.
ideasoc|Survivorship Bias|We see the planes that came back, the companies that succeeded and the books that survived, and forget the ones that didn't, so we draw the wrong lessons.|Abraham Wald||e|1943|In the Second World War, the US military wanted to add armour to bombers where returning planes were most shot up. The statistician Abraham Wald pointed out that those were the places a plane could be hit and still return. The armour should go where the survivors had no holes, because planes hit there never came back. Success stories mislead when the failures are invisible.
ideasoc|Schelling's Segregation Model|Even if people only mildly prefer having some neighbours like themselves, a city can end up sharply divided. Small individual preferences can add up to large patterns nobody wanted.|Thomas Schelling||e|1971|Schelling showed this with coins on a chessboard: each moves if fewer than a third of its neighbours match it. Within a few rounds, the board separates into clusters. The model showed how social patterns can emerge from simple choices, without any central plan, and that undoing them may need more than goodwill. Schelling won the Nobel Prize in economics in 2005.
ideaeast|Sympathetic Joy|Mudita is gladness at the happiness of others, the opposite of envy. In Buddhism it is one of four boundless states of heart, beside loving-kindness, compassion and equanimity.|Early Buddhist teaching||e|5th century BC onwards|The four brahmaviharas, the "divine abodes", are loving-kindness (metta), compassion (karuna), sympathetic joy (mudita) and equanimity (upekkha). Mudita is often said to be the hardest, because envy and comparison come so easily. It can be practised by wishing, quietly: may your good fortune continue. English borrowed schadenfreude for pleasure in others' misfortune, but has no common word for this.
ideaeast|The Constant Mean|The Confucian Doctrine of the Mean teaches balance: before joy, anger, sorrow and pleasure arise, the mind is centred; when they arise in due measure, there is harmony.|The Doctrine of the Mean (Zhongyong), attributed to Zisi||e|5th–3rd century BC|One of the Four Books of Confucianism, the Zhongyong describes the person who keeps to the middle way in everyday life, neither overdoing nor falling short, sincere and steady. Unlike Aristotle's golden mean, it is rooted in harmony with heaven and others. The text says the Way is not far from people; it is in ordinary conduct, done well.
ideaeast|The Way of Tea|Sen no Rikyū summed up the tea ceremony in four principles: harmony, respect, purity and tranquillity, wa kei sei jaku. A simple bowl of tea becomes a way of living.|Sen no Rikyū||e|16th century|Rikyū, tea master to the warlords Oda Nobunaga and Toyotomi Hideyoshi, made the ceremony humble: tiny rooms entered by crawling through a low door, rough handmade bowls, a single flower. In the tea room, all guests are equal. Each meeting is ichi-go ichi-e, once in a lifetime. His way of tea outlived the warlords he served, and is practised by his descendants' schools to this day.
ideaeast|Seva|Seva is selfless service, given without wanting anything in return. In Sikhism it is part of daily life: in the langar, the free community kitchen of every gurdwara, anyone of any faith is fed.|Guru Nanak and the Sikh tradition||e|15th century onwards|Guru Nanak founded the langar so that rich and poor, high and low caste, would sit on the floor and eat together. At the Golden Temple in Amritsar, volunteers cook and serve tens of thousands of meals a day. Seva is also important in Hindu traditions. The idea is that serving others humbles the ego and is itself a form of prayer.
ideaeast|Santosha|Santosha, contentment, is one of the personal practices of yoga: not resignation, but a quiet acceptance of what we have and who we are, from which deep happiness grows.|Patanjali, Yoga Sutras||e|about 2nd century BC–4th century AD|In the Yoga Sutras, santosha is one of the five niyamas, the inner observances. Patanjali says that from contentment comes the highest happiness. It does not mean giving up effort, but not letting our peace depend on the next thing we want. Many traditions agree: wealth is wanting what you have.
ideaeast|Hara Hachi Bu|Eat until you are eight parts out of ten full. Okinawans, among the longest-lived people in the world, traditionally say this before a meal.|Okinawan tradition (from Confucian teaching)||e|traditional|The phrase is said to come from Confucian teaching. Stopping before you feel full gives the stomach time to tell the brain it has had enough, which takes about twenty minutes. Researchers studying Okinawa's long-lived elders have linked the habit, along with plant-rich food, daily movement and close friendships, to their health. It is mindfulness at the table.
ideaphil|Hanlon's Razor|Never attribute to malice what can be adequately explained by stupidity, or, more kindly, by carelessness or misunderstanding.|Robert J. Hanlon (and many before him)||e|1980|The phrase was sent in by Robert Hanlon to a joke book of Murphy's Law variations, but the idea is old; Goethe wrote that misunderstandings and neglect cause more mischief than malice. It is a useful check on anger: most slights are not aimed at us. Assuming the best explanation first spares a great deal of conflict.
ideaphil|The Paradox of Tolerance|If a society is tolerant without limit, it may be destroyed by the intolerant. To stay tolerant, it must be ready to defend itself against intolerance.|Karl Popper||e|1945|Popper made the point in a footnote to The Open Society and Its Enemies, written in exile during the war. He added that intolerant ideas should be met with argument and public opinion as long as possible; only those who refuse argument and answer with violence should be suppressed. The paradox is still argued over today.
ideaphil|The Principle of Charity|When interpreting what someone says, assume they are reasonable, and choose the strongest, most sensible version of their argument before replying to it.|Neil L. Wilson; Donald Davidson||e|1959 onwards|The principle began in logic and the philosophy of language: to understand anyone at all, we must assume most of what they believe is true. In debate, it means arguing against the best version of a view, sometimes called steelmanning, rather than a weak caricature. It makes disagreements more honest, and sometimes shows we agree more than we thought.
ideaphil|Natality|Every birth brings someone new into the world, capable of beginning something that has never been before. For Hannah Arendt, this power to start anew is the root of freedom and hope.|Hannah Arendt||e|1958|In The Human Condition, Arendt set natality against the long philosophical focus on death. Because each person is a new beginning, human action is unpredictable: we can always start something unexpected, forgive, make promises. She quoted Augustine: "That a beginning be made, man was created." Her thought is a counterweight to despair about the world's direction.
ideaphil|The Essay as Attempt|Montaigne called his writings essais, "attempts" or "trials": not finished doctrines but a mind trying itself out on any subject, changing its view as it goes.|Michel de Montaigne||e|1580|Montaigne retired to his tower at 38 and wrote about friendship, cannibals, thumbs, sleep, smells and how to live, testing his judgement on each. He changed his mind openly and added to his essays for the rest of his life. He invented a new form: personal, curious and honest. Every essay since, including a school essay, is named after his attempts.
ideaphil|Philosophy as a Way of Life|For the ancient philosophers, philosophy was not a set of theories, but a practice for living: spiritual exercises in attention, self-examination and seeing things from above.|Pierre Hadot||e|1981|Hadot, a French historian of ancient thought, showed that Stoics, Epicureans and Platonists wrote to train themselves and their students, not to build systems. Marcus Aurelius's Meditations, for example, were exercises he did for himself. Hadot's work helped inspire today's interest in Stoicism and in philosophy as something you do, not just study.
ideaphil|Akrasia|Akrasia is weakness of will: knowing what is best and doing something else. Socrates thought it impossible; Aristotle, watching real people, knew it happens every day.|Socrates; Aristotle||e|4th century BC|In Plato's Protagoras, Socrates argues that no one does wrong knowingly: if we really knew what was best, we would do it. Aristotle, in the Nicomachean Ethics, answers that knowledge can be present but asleep, overpowered by desire in the moment, like the knowledge of a drunk. The puzzle is still alive in psychology, as procrastination, cravings and good intentions that melt away.
ideaphil|The Hedgehog and the Fox|"The fox knows many things, but the hedgehog knows one big thing." Some minds see the world through a single great idea; others gather many experiences that do not fit one system.|Archilochus; Isaiah Berlin||e|7th century BC; 1953|The line comes from a fragment of the Greek poet Archilochus. In 1953 the philosopher Isaiah Berlin made it the title of an essay on Tolstoy, sorting thinkers into hedgehogs (Plato, Dante, Dostoevsky, Nietzsche) and foxes (Aristotle, Shakespeare, Montaigne, Goethe). Tolstoy, he argued, was a fox by nature who longed to be a hedgehog. The psychologist Philip Tetlock later found that foxes make better forecasters.
ideaphil|The View from Nowhere|We can step back from our own point of view and try to see the world as no one in particular would. Objectivity is that effort, but it can never quite remove the one who is looking.|Thomas Nagel||e|1986|Nagel's book The View from Nowhere explores the tension between the subjective view, how things seem from inside a life, and the objective view, how they are from outside any life. Science climbs towards the second; but meaning, consciousness and the feeling that our lives matter keep pulling us back to the first. Nagel argued we must live with both, and that neither can absorb the other.
ideaphil|The Principle of Sufficient Reason|Nothing happens without a reason why it is so and not otherwise. Leibniz made this the foundation of his philosophy, and it leads straight to the oldest question: why is there something rather than nothing?|Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz||e|1714|Leibniz held two great principles: that nothing can contradict itself, and that nothing is without a sufficient reason. From the second he asked why the world exists at all, and answered: because God chose the best possible world. Schopenhauer wrote his first book on the principle; Heidegger called the question of why there is something rather than nothing the first of all questions.
ideaphil|The Elenchus|Socrates' method: ask someone what they believe, then ask question after question until the belief is tested, refined or shown to contradict itself, and both of you know a little better how little you know.|Socrates||e|5th century BC|In Plato's early dialogues, Socrates asks generals what courage is, priests what piety is, and teachers of rhetoric what virtue is. Each answer is examined until it collapses into puzzlement, aporia. The point was not humiliation but clearing the ground for real thinking. Law schools, therapists and good teachers still use the Socratic method: understanding by questioning, not telling.
ideamind|Self-Distancing|Looking at your own problem as if from the outside, even by talking to yourself by name or in the third person, makes it easier to think clearly and less painful to feel.|Ethan Kross||e|2010s|In experiments, people who reflected on a painful experience by asking "Why does Anna feel this way?" rather than "Why do I?" were calmer, wiser and less stuck in rumination. Kross calls the inner voice that loops on worries "chatter". Imagining advising a friend, picturing how you will see it in ten years, or zooming out like Marcus Aurelius's "view from above" all work the same way.
ideamind|Implementation Intentions|"When X happens, I will do Y." Deciding in advance exactly when, where and how you will act makes it much more likely that good intentions turn into action.|Peter Gollwitzer||e|1999|Gollwitzer found that people who form simple if-then plans ("If it is 7 a.m., I will walk for ten minutes") follow through far more often than those who just intend to. The plan hands control to the situation: when the moment comes, the action is triggered almost automatically, without needing willpower. Studies have found effects for exercise, studying, health screenings and voting.
ideamind|The Fresh Start Effect|New beginnings in time, such as a new year, a birthday, a Monday or the first day of a month, make people more likely to start pursuing their goals, because they feel like a clean break from a past self.|Hengchen Dai; Katherine Milkman||e|2014|Dai and Milkman found that visits to the gym, searches for "diet" and commitments to goals all rise after such landmarks. They separate time into chapters: the failures of the old self seem to belong to the last chapter. You can make your own fresh starts by marking a moment as a new beginning, though the effect fades unless habits are built.
ideamind|Mental Contrasting|Dreaming about a wished-for future feels good but can sap the energy to get there. Imagining the wish and then the main obstacle in the way, and planning for it, works much better.|Gabriele Oettingen||e|2000s|In Oettingen's studies, positive fantasies alone led to less effort and worse results: the mind had tasted success already. Her method, WOOP (Wish, Outcome, Obstacle, Plan), adds a realistic look at what stands in the way and an if-then plan to meet it. Hope, it turns out, works best when it looks squarely at reality.
ideamind|Giving Makes Us Happier|Spending money or time on others makes people happier than spending it on themselves, across rich and poor countries alike, and even small children show it.|Elizabeth Dunn; Michael Norton||e|2008|In Dunn and Norton's experiments, people given money to spend on someone else were happier at the end of the day than those told to spend it on themselves. Later studies found the same in many countries, and in toddlers giving treats away. The benefit is greatest when giving is freely chosen and we can see the difference it makes.
ideasci|The Wood Wide Web|Under the forest floor, threads of fungi link the roots of trees into a network through which water, sugars and chemical signals pass between them.|Suzanne Simard and others||e|1997|Most plants live in partnership with mycorrhizal fungi, which wrap or enter their roots and trade minerals for sugars. In 1997 Simard showed that carbon could move between trees through these fungal links. How much trees truly share or "communicate" is still debated, but the picture of a forest as a connected community, not a crowd of rivals, has changed how ecologists see woods.
ideasci|Murmurations|Thousands of starlings swirl as one shape-shifting cloud, with no leader. Each bird follows a few simple rules about its nearest neighbours, and the whole pattern emerges.|Craig Reynolds; Giorgio Parisi||e|1986; 2000s|In 1986 the computer scientist Craig Reynolds made lifelike flocks with three rules: steer away from crowding, match your neighbours' direction, move towards their centre. Studying real starlings, Giorgio Parisi's team found each bird tracks about seven neighbours, whatever the distance. Murmurations are a vivid example of emergence: complex order from simple local choices. Parisi won the Nobel Prize in physics in 2021.
ideasci|The Golden Record|In 1977 two Voyager spacecraft left Earth carrying a gold-plated record: greetings in 55 languages, music from Bach to Chuck Berry, whale song, a mother's kiss, and the brainwaves of a woman in love.|Carl Sagan and the Voyager team||e|1977|A committee led by Carl Sagan chose what to send. The record shows how to play it, and where the Sun is among the pulsars. Ann Druyan's brainwaves were recorded as she thought about history, and about falling in love. The Voyagers are now beyond the solar system, the farthest human-made objects; the chance anyone finds the records is tiny. They are a message in a bottle about who we hope we are.
ideasoc|The Wisdom of Crowds|The average of many independent guesses is often closer to the truth than almost any single expert, as long as people think for themselves and their errors cancel out.|Francis Galton; James Surowiecki||e|1907; 2004|At a country fair in 1906, Galton found that the median of 787 guesses of an ox's weight was within one per cent of the true figure. James Surowiecki's book of 2004 explored when crowds are wise: diversity of views, independence, and a good way to combine them. When people copy each other instead, crowds become herds, and bubbles and panics follow.
ideasoc|Social Prescribing|Doctors can prescribe not only pills but gardening clubs, choirs, walking groups, volunteering and art classes, because loneliness and lack of purpose make people ill.|National Health Service, UK||e|2010s onwards|Britain's NHS employs "link workers" who connect patients with community activities. The idea rests on decades of research showing that social connection affects health as much as smoking or exercise. Results are encouraging but still being studied. The idea is old, too: healers have long sent people to the mountains, the sea or good company.
ideaeast|Mottainai|A Japanese word of regret at waste: it is a pity to throw away something that still has use or worth, whether food, things, time or talents.|Japanese tradition (Buddhist roots)||e|traditional|Mottainai comes from Buddhist ideas of the interconnected worth of all things. Children are told "mottainai!" if they leave rice in their bowl. The Kenyan Nobel laureate Wangari Maathai adopted it in 2005 as a global slogan for reducing, reusing, recycling and respecting resources. It is gratitude turned into care: valuing what we already have.
ideaeast|Gaman|A Japanese word for enduring the seemingly unbearable with patience and dignity, without complaint, often for the sake of others.|Japanese tradition (Zen roots)||e|traditional|Gaman has Zen Buddhist roots. It was widely noticed after the 2011 earthquake and tsunami, when survivors queued calmly and shared what little they had. It also names the beautiful objects made by Japanese Americans in the internment camps of the Second World War from scraps. Gaman can become harmful if it means hiding pain; at its best it is quiet, shared resilience.
ideaeast|Nunchi|A Korean word for the art of sensing what others feel and think, reading the mood of a room, and responding with tact.|Korean tradition||e|traditional|Nunchi literally means "eye-measure". Korean children are taught it early: noticing who is tired, who is uncomfortable, when to speak and when to stay quiet. It is close to emotional intelligence, but with a stronger sense of harmony in the group. Someone with quick nunchi makes everyone feel at ease; someone without it is said to have "no nunchi".
ideaeast|Jeong|A Korean word for the deep, sticky affection and loyalty that grows between people over time, through shared life, even between rivals or with places and things.|Korean tradition||e|traditional|Jeong is not chosen like love; it accumulates, through years of meals shared, hardships endured and small kindnesses. It can bind a neighbourhood, an old couple, or a person to their childhood home, and it can make leaving hard. Koreans say jeong is shown more in deeds, like the last rice cake saved for you, than in words.
ideaart|Vanitas|Still-life paintings of skulls, guttering candles, wilting flowers and hourglasses among books and fine things, reminding the viewer that all of it passes.|Dutch and Flemish painters||e|17th century|The name comes from Ecclesiastes: vanitas vanitatum, "vanity of vanities". Painters like Pieter Claesz and Harmen Steenwyck arranged precious objects beside symbols of time and mortality. The paintings were often bought by the very merchants whose riches they showed. Their message is not gloom but proportion: hold lightly what you cannot keep.
ideaart|The Season Word|Almost every traditional haiku contains a kigo, a word that places it in a season: frogs, cherry blossom, autumn moon, snow. One word brings a whole world of feeling with it.|Japanese haiku tradition||e|17th century onwards|Poets keep almanacs (saijiki) listing thousands of season words with their meanings and example poems. "Autumn wind" carries loneliness; "spring rain" softness. Bashō's frog jumping into the old pond is a spring word. The kigo connects a tiny poem to the turning year and to every poem written with the same word before it.
ideaart|Ragas of the Hours|In Indian classical music, each raga, a set of notes and phrases with its own mood, belongs to a time of day or a season: there are morning ragas, evening ragas and ragas of the monsoon.|Indian classical tradition||e|traditional|A raga such as Bhairav belongs to dawn, Yaman to early evening, Malkauns to the deep night, and the Malhar ragas to the rains; legend says the singer Tansen could make rain fall by singing one. Musicians still largely follow the time theory. It reflects a belief that music should be in harmony with the rhythm of nature and the listener's state.
ideaart|Ubi Sunt|"Where are they now?" Poets of many centuries have asked where the people of the past have gone, to remember them and to feel how brief life is.|Medieval Latin poets; François Villon||e|Middle Ages|The phrase comes from the Latin "Ubi sunt qui ante nos fuerunt?": where are those who were before us? Villon asked "Where are the snows of yesteryear?" in his Ballad of the Ladies of Times Past (1461). The Old English Wanderer asks where the horse and the rider have gone. The question is sad, but it is also tender: it keeps the dead in mind.
ideamath|The Golden Angle|Sunflowers, pine cones and daisies place each new seed about 137.5° around from the last. This golden angle packs seeds most evenly and makes the spirals we see, in Fibonacci numbers.|Phyllotaxis; Douady and Couder||e|19th–20th centuries|Count the spirals in a sunflower head and you usually get neighbouring Fibonacci numbers, such as 34 and 55. The golden angle divides a circle in the golden ratio, so no two seeds ever line up exactly, leaving no gaps. In 1992 the physicists Stéphane Douady and Yves Couder reproduced the patterns with drops of magnetised fluid, showing they arise naturally from simple growth rules.
ideamath|Kepler's Snowflake|As a New Year's gift for a friend in 1611, Johannes Kepler wrote a little book asking why snowflakes always have six sides, and opened a question about how nature packs things together.|Johannes Kepler||e|1611|In On the Six-Cornered Snowflake, Kepler looked at honeycombs and pomegranate seeds and guessed that the closest way to pack equal spheres is the way grocers stack oranges. This "Kepler conjecture" was proved only in 1998, by Thomas Hales, with the help of computers. We now know the six sides of snow come from the way water molecules bond, as Kepler suspected without being able to see it.
ideamath|Turing Patterns|Alan Turing showed how two chemicals, one spreading fast and one slowly, reacting together, can produce spots and stripes from an even start: a possible secret behind leopard spots and zebra stripes.|Alan Turing||e|1952|In his paper The Chemical Basis of Morphogenesis, written two years before his death, Turing proposed that patterns in living things can form spontaneously from reaction and diffusion. For decades biologists were doubtful, but such patterns have since been found in the spacing of fingers, the stripes of fish, the ridges of the mouth's palate and hair follicles. Pure mathematics turned out to describe how a body takes shape.
mythworld|Rusalka|In Slavic folklore, rusalki are water spirits, young women with long loose hair who live in rivers and lakes and come out on summer nights to dance in the meadows and swing in the birches.|Slavic folklore||e|Eastern Europe|Rusalki were said to be the spirits of girls who had died young, often near water; during Rusalka Week in early summer, villagers left them gifts and avoided swimming. They could be dangerous, luring people into the water, or kind, bringing rain for the crops. Dvořák's opera Rusalka (1901), with its "Song to the Moon", turned the legend into a story of longing between two worlds.
mythworld|Saulė, the Sun Mother|In Baltic myth, the Sun is a goddess, Saulė, who drives a copper chariot across the sky by day and sails home in a golden boat across the sea at night.|Baltic myth||e|Lithuania and Latvia|Saulė was married to Mėnulis, the Moon, who fell in love with the Morning Star, Aušrinė; in anger Perkūnas, the thunder god, cut the Moon in two, which is why it waxes and wanes. Latvian and Lithuanian folk songs (dainas) still sing of Saulė. At midsummer, Rasos or Jāņi, people stay up all night with bonfires and flowers to greet her.
mythworld|Mami Wata|Mami Wata, "Mother Water", is a water spirit honoured across West and Central Africa and its diaspora: beautiful, wealthy, often shown with a snake, bringing healing, good fortune and danger.|West and Central African tradition||e|West and Central Africa and the Americas|Her followers wear white and red, and offer perfume, sweets and mirrors at the water's edge. Her popular image owes something to a 19th-century German print of a snake charmer that travelled widely in Africa. She embodies the sea's gifts and risks, and the riches and dangers of trade across it. She is still a living tradition in Nigeria, Benin, Togo and Ghana.
mythworld|The Seven Sisters|In many Aboriginal Australian songlines, the Pleiades are seven sisters fleeing across the land and up into the sky from a man who pursues them, and their journey maps waterholes and sacred places across the desert.|Aboriginal Australian songlines||e|Australia, many thousands of years|The Seven Sisters story is told across vast distances, by many peoples in different languages, each holding part of the route. In 2017 a major exhibition at the National Museum of Australia brought paintings and songs from these traditions together. Many cultures around the world, from Greece to North America, also see seven sisters in the Pleiades.
mythworld|Dangun|A bear who wished to become human lived in a cave for a hundred days on mugwort and garlic, and became a woman. She married the son of the heavenly king, and their son Dangun founded the first Korean kingdom.|Korean myth||e|traditionally 2333 BC|A tiger tried the same trial but gave up; the bear endured. Dangun Wanggeom founded Gojoseon, the first Korean state, according to the Samguk yusa, written down in the 13th century. Korea celebrates his founding of the nation on National Foundation Day, 3 October. The bear's patience is still held up as a Korean virtue.
mythworld|The Dragon Lord and the Fairy|Lạc Long Quân, a dragon lord of the sea, married Âu Cơ, a fairy of the mountains. She laid a hundred eggs, and from them hatched a hundred children, the ancestors of the Vietnamese people.|Vietnamese myth||e|Vietnam|Being of the sea and the mountains, the two could not live together. Fifty children went with their father to the coast and fifty with their mother to the highlands, and the eldest son became the first of the Hùng kings. Vietnamese people still call each other đồng bào, "from the same womb", recalling that everyone came from one sac of a hundred eggs.
mythworld|Malakas and Maganda|In a Philippine creation story, a bird pecked open a bamboo stalk, and out came the first man and woman: Malakas, "strong", and Maganda, "beautiful".|Tagalog myth||e|the Philippines|In one telling, the sea and the sky quarrelled, and a bird, with nowhere to land, pecked at a bamboo floating on the water. The two first people stepped out of the split stalk side by side, equal from the start. Their story appears in Philippine art and public statues, a reminder of the islands' links to bamboo, birds and the sea.
mythworld|The Simurgh|The Simurgh is a vast, wise bird of Persian myth, nesting in the Tree of Life. In Attar's poem, thirty birds seek her as their king, and find at the end that si murgh, "thirty birds", is themselves.|Persian myth; Attar, The Conference of the Birds||e|12th century|In Ferdowsi's Shahnameh the Simurgh raises the abandoned baby Zal and later helps his son Rustam with her healing feathers. Attar's Sufi poem turns her into a symbol of God: the hoopoe leads the birds through seven valleys, and most give up; the thirty who arrive see themselves reflected in the Simurgh. The search for the divine ends in finding it within.
mythworld|Viracocha|In Inca myth, Viracocha rose from Lake Titicaca, made the sun, moon and stars, and shaped people from stone. Then he walked across the land teaching, and vanished westward over the sea.|Inca myth||e|the Andes|Viracocha first made a race of giants, then destroyed them with a flood when they displeased him, and made humans anew. Dressed as a beggar, he wandered the world teaching people how to live, and was sometimes not recognised. At the coast he walked out across the waves and disappeared. The Spanish chroniclers later recorded the story from Inca accounts.
mythworld|Pachamama|Pachamama, Mother Earth, is honoured across the Andes: the land that feeds, shelters and must be given back to. Before eating or drinking, people still offer her the first drops.|Andean tradition||e|the Andes|Pacha means world, time and space, and mama means mother. In August, when the earth is thought to be hungry before planting, families bury offerings of food, coca leaves and chicha for her. Ecuador's constitution of 2008 gave rights to nature, naming Pachamama. The idea is reciprocity: we take from the earth and must return the gift.
mythancient|The Bennu Bird|The Bennu, a heron of Egyptian myth, flew over the first waters and gave the cry that began time. It was linked to the sun's rising and renewal, and the Greeks knew it as the phoenix.|Egyptian myth||e|ancient Egypt|The Bennu perched on the benben, the first mound of land to rise from the waters of chaos at Heliopolis. Each dawn the sun rose again, like the Bennu. The Greek historian Herodotus heard of a bird that came to Heliopolis every 500 years, and the legend grew into the phoenix that rises from its own ashes, a symbol of renewal for many cultures since.
mythnorse|The Norns|Three women, the Norns, sit at the foot of the world-tree Yggdrasil beside the Well of Urd. They water its roots each day and spin, or carve, the fates of all beings.|The Poetic and Prose Eddas||e|13th century|Their names are often given as Urd (what has been), Verdandi (what is coming into being) and Skuld (what shall be). Even the gods were subject to fate. The Old English word wyrd, fate, is related to Urd, and through it the "weird sisters" of Shakespeare's Macbeth. The Norns keep the tree alive even as fate unfolds.
mythgreek|Halcyon Days|When Alcyone's husband Ceyx drowned at sea, she threw herself into the waves after him, and the gods turned them both into kingfishers. Each winter, her father the wind god calms the sea for seven days so she can nest.|Ovid, Metamorphoses; Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|Alcyone saw Ceyx's death in a dream sent by the goddess of sleep, and found his body on the shore. Out of pity the gods changed the faithful pair into birds, the halcyons. The calm days around the winter solstice, when the Greeks believed kingfishers nested on the sea, were called the halcyon days. We still use the phrase for a peaceful, happy time.
mythgreek|Chiron, the Wounded Healer|Chiron, wisest of the centaurs, taught heroes medicine, music and archery. Struck by a poisoned arrow, he could not heal his own wound, and became the image of the healer who knows pain from within.|Greek myth||e|ancient Greece|Chiron raised Asclepius, the god of medicine, and taught Achilles, Jason and Heracles. When Heracles accidentally wounded him with an arrow dipped in the Hydra's blood, the immortal Chiron could neither heal nor die, until he gave his immortality to free Prometheus. Zeus set him among the stars as the constellation Centaurus. Carl Jung used the "wounded healer" to describe therapists whose own wounds help them understand others.
mythworld|The Sun and the Moon Siblings|In an Inuit story, the Sun is a sister, Malina, and the Moon her brother, Anningan. After a quarrel she fled into the sky with a torch, and he chases her for ever, growing thin from hunger as he forgets to eat.|Inuit myth||e|Greenland and the Arctic|Anningan lit his own torch in the chase, but it sputtered out, leaving only embers, which is why the moon is pale. When he grows too thin he disappears for a few days to hunt, and the moon is dark, before returning to the chase. The story explains the moon's phases and the way sun and moon follow each other across the sky.
